Day 050 / Unit 4: Use Factors and Multiples to Explain Structure

6.NS.B.4a, 6.NS.B.4b

I can use Factors and Multiples to Explain Structure, and explain and check my reasoning.

enVision Topic 3: Numeric and Algebraic Expressions | Spectrum companion Chapter 1

List factor pairs or use prime factorizations. The greatest factor shared by both numbers is the GCF. For identical kits with no leftovers, the GCF gives the greatest number of kits. List positive multiples of both numbers. The smallest shared multiple is the LCM. Repeating events that begin together next occur together after this many time units.

Worked example

Find the GCF of 8 and 12. Show factor pairs or prime factors.

Common factors: 1, 2, 4; greatest is 4.

4

Worked example

Two signals flash together now and then every 2 and 9 seconds. Select all true statements. A. They next flash together after 18 seconds. B. They next flash together after 1 seconds. C. 36 seconds is also a time they flash together. D. The next shared time is 19 seconds.

List multiples or use prime factors. The least shared positive multiple is 18; multiples of 18 are later shared times.

A, C - They next flash together after 18 seconds.; 36 seconds is also a time they flash together.

  1. Find the GCF of 12 and 16. Show factor pairs or prime factors.

  2. Find the LCM of 5 and 4 by listing multiples.

  3. Find the GCF of 6 and 9. Show factor pairs or prime factors.

  4. Find the LCM of 12 and 6 by listing multiples.

  5. Find the GCF of 60 and 84. Show factor pairs or prime factors.

  6. Find the LCM of 3 and 8 by listing multiples.

  7. Make the greatest possible number of identical kits from 70 pencils and 100 erasers with no leftovers. Find the number of kits and each kit’s contents.

  8. Two signals flash together now and then every 3 and 11 seconds. Select all true statements. A. 66 seconds is also a time they flash together. B. They next flash together after 1 seconds. C. The next shared time is 34 seconds. D. They next flash together after 33 seconds.

  9. Select all true statements about 36 and 54. Explain why the greatest factor fits the kit situation. A. 90 divides both counts. B. 19 is their greatest common factor. C. 18 divides both 36 and 54. D. 18 is the largest number of identical no-leftover kits from 36 pencils and 54 erasers.

  10. Two lights flash together now, then every 8 and 3 seconds. When do they next flash together? Explain.

Answer key
  1. 4

    Common factors: 1, 2, 4; greatest is 4.

  2. 20

    The first positive multiple shared by 5 and 4 is 20.

  3. 3

    Common factors: 1, 3; greatest is 3.

  4. 12

    The first positive multiple shared by 12 and 6 is 12.

  5. 12

    Common factors: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12; greatest is 12.

  6. 24

    The first positive multiple shared by 3 and 8 is 24.

  7. 10 kits; 7 pencils and 10 erasers each

    GCF(70, 100) = 10; divide both counts by 10.

  8. A, D - 66 seconds is also a time they flash together.; They next flash together after 33 seconds.

    List multiples or use prime factors. The least shared positive multiple is 33; multiples of 33 are later shared times.

  9. C, D - 18 divides both 36 and 54.; 18 is the largest number of identical no-leftover kits from 36 pencils and 54 erasers.

    GCF(36, 54) = 18. Divide each count by 18: 2 pencils and 3 erasers per kit. A larger shared divisor is impossible.

  10. 24 seconds

    The first positive multiple shared by 8 and 3 is 24.

Day 051 / Unit 4: Factor Sums with the Distributive Property

6.NS.B.4c

I can factor a sum using the greatest common factor.

enVision Topic 3: Numeric and Algebraic Expressions | Spectrum companion Chapter 1

Find the GCF of the two addends. Divide each addend by that factor and place the quotients inside parentheses. The inside numbers should share no common factor greater than 1.

Worked example

Factor 8 + 12 using its GCF. Make the inside numbers relatively prime.

GCF = 4; 8 = 4 × 2 and 12 = 4 × 3.

4(2 + 3)

Worked example

Select all expressions equal to 36 + 18. A. 6(6 + 3) B. 55 C. 6 × 10 D. 36 + 18

Distribute 6 to both terms: 6 × 6 + 6 × 3 = 54. The other choices are 6 or 1 too large.

A, D - 6(6 + 3); 36 + 18

  1. Factor 6 + 9 using its GCF. Make the inside numbers relatively prime.

  2. Factor 10 + 15 using its GCF. Make the inside numbers relatively prime.

  3. Factor 12 + 16 using its GCF. Make the inside numbers relatively prime.

  4. Factor 28 + 42 using its GCF. Make the inside numbers relatively prime.

  5. Factor 48 + 72 using its GCF. Make the inside numbers relatively prime.

  6. Factor 12 + 18 using its GCF. Make the inside numbers relatively prime.

  7. Factor 18 + 30 using its GCF. Make the inside numbers relatively prime.

  8. Select all expressions equal to 6 + 6. A. 13 B. 2(3 + 3) C. 2 × 7 D. 6 + 6

  9. Select all expressions equal to 25 + 15. A. 5 × 9 B. 5(5 + 3) C. 41 D. 25 + 15

  10. Factor 70 + 100 using its GCF. Make the inside numbers relatively prime.

Answer key
  1. 3(2 + 3)

    GCF = 3; 6 = 3 × 2 and 9 = 3 × 3.

  2. 5(2 + 3)

    GCF = 5; 10 = 5 × 2 and 15 = 5 × 3.

  3. 4(3 + 4)

    GCF = 4; 12 = 4 × 3 and 16 = 4 × 4.

  4. 14(2 + 3)

    GCF = 14; 28 = 14 × 2 and 42 = 14 × 3.

  5. 24(2 + 3)

    GCF = 24; 48 = 24 × 2 and 72 = 24 × 3.

  6. 6(2 + 3)

    GCF = 6; 12 = 6 × 2 and 18 = 6 × 3.

  7. 6(3 + 5)

    GCF = 6; 18 = 6 × 3 and 30 = 6 × 5.

  8. B, D - 2(3 + 3); 6 + 6

    Distribute 2 to both terms: 2 × 3 + 2 × 3 = 12. The other choices are 2 or 1 too large.

  9. B, D - 5(5 + 3); 25 + 15

    Distribute 5 to both terms: 5 × 5 + 5 × 3 = 40. The other choices are 5 or 1 too large.

  10. 10(7 + 10)

    GCF = 10; 70 = 10 × 7 and 100 = 10 × 10.

Day 052 / Unit 4: Identify Parts of Expressions

6.EE.A.2b

I can name terms, coefficients, factors, and grouped parts.

enVision Topic 3: Numeric and Algebraic Expressions | Spectrum companion Chapter 5

Addition and subtraction separate terms. A coefficient multiplies a variable; a constant has no variable. A parenthesized sum can be one factor of a larger product, so describe both the inside and the whole expression.

Worked example

For 4x + 7y + 3, list terms, coefficients, and the constant.

Terms are separated by addition; coefficients multiply variables.

Terms 4x, 7y, 3; coefficients 4, 7; constant 3

Worked example

For the expression 6(x + 2) + 4, select all true statements. A. 6 and (x + 2) are factors of the first term. B. 2 is the coefficient of x in the fully expanded expression. C. 4 is a constant term. D. The entire expression is a product with 4.

The outer sum has two terms. The first term is a product with two factors. Expanding gives 6x + 12 + 4, so the coefficient is 6.

A, C - 6 and (x + 2) are factors of the first term.; 4 is a constant term.

  1. For 4x + 4y + 6, list terms, coefficients, and the constant.

  2. For 3x + 7y + 5, list terms, coefficients, and the constant.

  3. For (6x + 7) ÷ 3, name the outer operation, the dividend as a single entity, and the divisor. Identify the sum inside the parentheses.

  4. For 2x + 5y + 2, list terms, coefficients, and the constant.

  5. For (4x + 4) ÷ 4, name the outer operation, the dividend as a single entity, and the divisor. Identify the sum inside the parentheses.

  6. For 6x + 5y + 5, list terms, coefficients, and the constant.

  7. For (2x + 3) ÷ 5, name the outer operation, the dividend as a single entity, and the divisor. Identify the sum inside the parentheses.

  8. For the expression 7(x + 4) + 2, select all true statements. A. The entire expression is a product with 2. B. 2 is a constant term. C. 4 is the coefficient of x in the fully expanded expression. D. 7 and (x + 4) are factors of the first term.

  9. For the expression 3(x + 4) + 3, select all true statements. A. 4 is the coefficient of x in the fully expanded expression. B. 3 is a constant term. C. 3 and (x + 4) are factors of the first term. D. The entire expression is a product with 3.

  10. In 7(x + 4), identify the outer operation, both factors, and the operation inside the grouped factor.

Answer key
  1. Terms 4x, 4y, 6; coefficients 4, 4; constant 6

    Terms are separated by addition; coefficients multiply variables.

  2. Terms 3x, 7y, 5; coefficients 3, 7; constant 5

    Terms are separated by addition; coefficients multiply variables.

  3. Outer quotient; dividend (6x + 7); divisor 3; inner sum 6x + 7

    The grouped sum is one complete quantity being divided. Within it, the terms are 6x and 7.

  4. Terms 2x, 5y, 2; coefficients 2, 5; constant 2

    Terms are separated by addition; coefficients multiply variables.

  5. Outer quotient; dividend (4x + 4); divisor 4; inner sum 4x + 4

    The grouped sum is one complete quantity being divided. Within it, the terms are 4x and 4.

  6. Terms 6x, 5y, 5; coefficients 6, 5; constant 5

    Terms are separated by addition; coefficients multiply variables.

  7. Outer quotient; dividend (2x + 3); divisor 5; inner sum 2x + 3

    The grouped sum is one complete quantity being divided. Within it, the terms are 2x and 3.

  8. B, D - 2 is a constant term.; 7 and (x + 4) are factors of the first term.

    The outer sum has two terms. The first term is a product with two factors. Expanding gives 7x + 28 + 2, so the coefficient is 7.

  9. B, C - 3 is a constant term.; 3 and (x + 4) are factors of the first term.

    The outer sum has two terms. The first term is a product with two factors. Expanding gives 3x + 12 + 3, so the coefficient is 3.

  10. Outer product; factors 7 and (x + 4); inner sum

    The complete sum (x + 4) is one factor multiplied by 7.

Day 053 / Unit 4: Write Expressions from Words

6.EE.A.2a, 6.EE.B.6

I can translate words and situations into expressions.

enVision Topic 3: Numeric and Algebraic Expressions | Spectrum companion Chapter 5

Define the variable first. Use parentheses for a sum or difference that must stay together. Read subtraction carefully: five less than n means n - 5, while five minus n means 5 - n.

Worked example

Write an expression for 2 less than n.

Start with n, then subtract the named amount.

n - 2

Worked example

A student buys x markers at $4 each and y folders at $5 each, then uses one $3 coupon. Part A: enter an expression for the total cost. Part B: explain why the coupon is subtracted only once.

Marker cost is 4x; folder cost is 5y. Add these subtotals, then subtract the one fixed coupon of 3. An expression does not need an equals sign.

4x + 5y - 3

  1. Write an expression for 7 less than n.

  2. Write an expression for 5 less than n.

  3. Write an expression for 8 less than n.

  4. Write an expression for 6 times the sum of x and 4.

  5. Write an expression for 7 times the sum of x and 6.

  6. Write an expression for 3 times the sum of x and 7.

  7. A ticket costs $4. A group buys n tickets and pays a single $7 booking fee. Define n and write the total-cost expression. Evaluate it for n = 4.

  8. A student buys x markers at $6 each and y folders at $4 each, then uses one $3 coupon. Part A: enter an expression for the total cost. Part B: explain why the coupon is subtracted only once.

  9. A student buys x markers at $4 each and y folders at $2 each, then uses one $3 coupon. Part A: enter an expression for the total cost. Part B: explain why the coupon is subtracted only once.

  10. A ticket costs $7. A group buys n tickets and pays a single $3 booking fee. Define n and write the total-cost expression. Evaluate it for n = 3.

Answer key
  1. n - 7

    Start with n, then subtract the named amount.

  2. n - 5

    Start with n, then subtract the named amount.

  3. n - 8

    Start with n, then subtract the named amount.

  4. 6(x + 4)

    Group the sum before multiplying.

  5. 7(x + 6)

    Group the sum before multiplying.

  6. 3(x + 7)

    Group the sum before multiplying.

  7. n = ticket count; 4n + 7; $23

    Ticket cost is 4 × n; add the one-time fee 7.

  8. 6x + 4y - 3

    Marker cost is 6x; folder cost is 4y. Add these subtotals, then subtract the one fixed coupon of 3. An expression does not need an equals sign.

  9. 4x + 2y - 3

    Marker cost is 4x; folder cost is 2y. Add these subtotals, then subtract the one fixed coupon of 3. An expression does not need an equals sign.

  10. n = ticket count; 7n + 3; $24

    Ticket cost is 7 × n; add the one-time fee 3.

Day 054 / Unit 4: Evaluate Expressions and Formulas

6.EE.A.2c

I can substitute values and evaluate expressions or formulas.

enVision Topic 3: Numeric and Algebraic Expressions | Spectrum companion Chapter 5

Replace every variable with its assigned value, using parentheses. Apply the order of operations. In formulas, label the answer with the appropriate measurement units.

Worked example

Evaluate 4x + 4 for x = 6.

Substitute first: 4 × 6 + 4 = 28.

28

Worked example

Let x = 5/2 and y = 5. Evaluate 4x + y². Part A: enter the value. Part B: show substitution and the order of operations.

Substitute: 4 × (5/2) + 5². The product is 10 and the square is 25. Add to get 35.

35

  1. Evaluate 8x + 5 for x = 2.

  2. Evaluate 8x + 6 for x = 2.

  3. Evaluate 4x + 6 for x = 3.

  4. Evaluate x^2 + 5y for x = 6 and y = 4.

  5. Evaluate x^2 + 3y for x = 3 and y = 3.

  6. Evaluate x^2 + 2y for x = 4 and y = 3.

  7. A rectangle has length x + 6 and width 4. Use A = lw to write its area expression and find A when x = 6.

  8. Let x = 3/2 and y = 5. Evaluate 3x + y². Part A: enter the value. Part B: show substitution and the order of operations.

  9. Let x = 1 and y = 5. Evaluate 7x + y². Part A: enter the value. Part B: show substitution and the order of operations.

  10. A rectangle has length x + 6 and width 6. Use A = lw to write its area expression and find A when x = 2.

Answer key
  1. 21

    Substitute first: 8 × 2 + 5 = 21.

  2. 22

    Substitute first: 8 × 2 + 6 = 22.

  3. 18

    Substitute first: 4 × 3 + 6 = 18.

  4. 56

    Substitute first: 6^2 + 5 × 4 = 56.

  5. 18

    Substitute first: 3^2 + 3 × 3 = 18.

  6. 22

    Substitute first: 4^2 + 2 × 3 = 22.

  7. A = 4(x + 6); 48 square units

    Substitute x = 6: 4(6 + 6) = 48.

  8. 59/2

    Substitute: 3 × (3/2) + 5². The product is 9/2 and the square is 25. Add to get 59/2.

  9. 32

    Substitute: 7 × (1) + 5². The product is 7 and the square is 25. Add to get 32.

  10. A = 6(x + 6); 48 square units

    Substitute x = 2: 6(2 + 6) = 48.

12x

Day 058 / Unit 4: Identify Equivalent Expressions

6.EE.A.4

I can justify whether expressions are equivalent.

enVision Topic 3: Numeric and Algebraic Expressions | Spectrum companion Chapter 5

Use operation properties to rewrite an expression. Matching values at one input do not prove equivalence for all inputs. A single counterexample is enough to show two expressions are not equivalent.

Worked example

Are 6(x + 7) and 6x + 42 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.

6(x + 7) = 6x + 42. This matches.

Yes; distribution gives 6x + 42

Worked example

Select all expressions equivalent to 6(x + 3). A. 9x B. 6x + 18 C. 6x + 3 D. 18 + 6x

Distribute 6 to both terms to get 6x + 18. Reversing the addends gives the same value. The other expressions fail, for example at x = 0.

B, D - 6x + 18; 18 + 6x

  1. Are 7(x + 2) and 7x + 2 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.

  2. Are 2(x + 5) and 2x + 10 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.

  3. Are 8(x + 7) and 8x + 7 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.

  4. Are 8(x + 2) and 8x + 16 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.

  5. Are 3(x + 2) and 3x + 2 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.

  6. Are 2(x + 7) and 2x + 14 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.

  7. Are 6(x + 6) and 6x + 6 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.

  8. Select all expressions equivalent to 7(x + 4). A. 11x B. 7x + 28 C. 7x + 4 D. 28 + 7x

  9. Select all expressions equivalent to 4(x + 6). A. 10x B. 4x + 6 C. 4x + 24 D. 24 + 4x

  10. Are 3(x + 6) and 3x + 18 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.

Answer key
  1. No; at x = 0, values are 14 and 2

    7(x + 2) = 7x + 14. The constants 14 and 2 differ.

  2. Yes; distribution gives 2x + 10

    2(x + 5) = 2x + 10. This matches.

  3. No; at x = 0, values are 56 and 7

    8(x + 7) = 8x + 56. The constants 56 and 7 differ.

  4. Yes; distribution gives 8x + 16

    8(x + 2) = 8x + 16. This matches.

  5. No; at x = 0, values are 6 and 2

    3(x + 2) = 3x + 6. The constants 6 and 2 differ.

  6. Yes; distribution gives 2x + 14

    2(x + 7) = 2x + 14. This matches.

  7. No; at x = 0, values are 36 and 6

    6(x + 6) = 6x + 36. The constants 36 and 6 differ.

  8. B, D - 7x + 28; 28 + 7x

    Distribute 7 to both terms to get 7x + 28. Reversing the addends gives the same value. The other expressions fail, for example at x = 0.

  9. C, D - 4x + 24; 24 + 4x

    Distribute 4 to both terms to get 4x + 24. Reversing the addends gives the same value. The other expressions fail, for example at x = 0.

  10. Yes; distribution gives 3x + 18

    3(x + 6) = 3x + 18. This matches.

Day 059 / Unit 4: Apply Expression Structure

6.EE.A.1, 6.EE.A.2c, 6.NS.B.4c, 6.EE.A.4

I can apply Expression Structure, and explain and check my reasoning.

enVision Topic 3: Numeric and Algebraic Expressions | Spectrum companion Chapters 5, 1

Work inside parentheses, then evaluate exponents. Multiply and divide from left to right; then add and subtract from left to right. Multiplication does not automatically come before division. Replace every variable with its assigned value, using parentheses. Apply the order of operations. In formulas, label the answer with the appropriate measurement units. Find the GCF of the two addends. Divide each addend by that factor and place the quotients inside parentheses. The inside numbers should share no common factor greater than 1. Use operation properties to rewrite an expression. Matching values at one input do not prove equivalence for all inputs. A single counterexample is enough to show two expressions are not equivalent.

Worked example

Evaluate 8 + 3 × 6. Show the intermediate steps.

Use parentheses and exponents first, then × and ÷ left to right, then + and -. Result: 26.

26

Worked example

Select all expressions equivalent to 7(x + 4). A. 11x B. 7x + 28 C. 7x + 4 D. 28 + 7x

Distribute 7 to both terms to get 7x + 28. Reversing the addends gives the same value. The other expressions fail, for example at x = 0.

B, D - 7x + 28; 28 + 7x

  1. Evaluate 2 + 5 × 5. Show the intermediate steps.

  2. Evaluate 4x + 3 for x = 2.

  3. Factor 8 + 12 using its GCF. Make the inside numbers relatively prime.

  4. Are 7(x + 3) and 7x + 21 equivalent for every x? Use a property or a counterexample.

  5. Evaluate (8 + 6) × 3^2. Show the intermediate steps.

  6. Evaluate x^2 + 8y for x = 5 and y = 7.

  7. Factor 32 + 48 using its GCF. Make the inside numbers relatively prime.

  8. Select all expressions equivalent to 3(x + 2). A. 6 + 3x B. 5x C. 3x + 6 D. 3x + 2

  9. Evaluate 4² + 2(8 - 3). Part A: enter the value. Part B: a student begins by adding 4² and 2; explain why that changes the expression.

  10. A rectangle has length x + 4 and width 7. Use A = lw to write its area expression and find A when x = 4.

Answer key
  1. 27

    Use parentheses and exponents first, then × and ÷ left to right, then + and -. Result: 27.

  2. 11

    Substitute first: 4 × 2 + 3 = 11.

  3. 4(2 + 3)

    GCF = 4; 8 = 4 × 2 and 12 = 4 × 3.

  4. Yes; distribution gives 7x + 21

    7(x + 3) = 7x + 21. This matches.

  5. 126

    Use parentheses and exponents first, then × and ÷ left to right, then + and -. Result: 126.

  6. 81

    Substitute first: 5^2 + 8 × 7 = 81.

  7. 16(2 + 3)

    GCF = 16; 32 = 16 × 2 and 48 = 16 × 3.

  8. A, C - 6 + 3x; 3x + 6

    Distribute 3 to both terms to get 3x + 6. Reversing the addends gives the same value. The other expressions fail, for example at x = 0.

  9. 26

    Parentheses: 8 - 3 = 5. Exponent: 4² = 16. Multiply: 2 × 5 = 10. Add: 16 + 10 = 26. The product must be completed before the final sum.

  10. A = 7(x + 4); 56 square units

    Substitute x = 4: 7(4 + 4) = 56.

Day 060 / Unit 4: Unit 4 Test

6.NS.C.5, 6.NS.C.6a, 6.NS.C.6c, 6.EE.A.1, 6.EE.A.2c, 6.NS.B.4a, 6.NS.B.4b, 6.NS.B.4c, 6.EE.A.2b, 6.EE.A.2a, 6.EE.B.6, 6.EE.A.3, 6.EE.A.4

I can choose a model, solve accurately, explain my reasoning, and check the result.

enVision Topic 3: Numeric and Algebraic Expressions | Spectrum companion Chapter 5

  1. A marker is 28 m above sea level. Write its signed elevation and explain what elevation 0 means.

  2. Find the opposite of -8. Show both values on a number line.

  3. Draw a horizontal or vertical number line. Plot and label -8, 0, and 7.

  4. Draw a horizontal or vertical number line. Plot and label -5, 0, and 3.

  5. Write 4^2 as repeated multiplication and evaluate.

  6. Evaluate 5 + 4 × 4. Show the intermediate steps.

  7. Find the GCF of 10 and 15. Show factor pairs or prime factors.

  8. Find the LCM of 8 and 9 by listing multiples.

  9. Find the GCF of 24 and 36. Show factor pairs or prime factors.

  10. Factor 28 + 42 using its GCF. Make the inside numbers relatively prime.

  11. For (5x + 7) ÷ 2, name the outer operation, the dividend as a single entity, and the divisor. Identify the sum inside the parentheses.

  12. A student buys x markers at $4 each and y folders at $3 each, then uses one $2 coupon. Part A: enter an expression for the total cost. Part B: explain why the coupon is subtracted only once.

  13. Let x = 1 and y = 3. Evaluate 4x + y². Part A: enter the value. Part B: show substitution and the order of operations.

  14. Select all expressions equivalent to 7(x + 6). A. 42 + 7x B. 13x C. 7x + 6 D. 7x + 42

  15. Select all expressions equivalent to 6(x + 5). A. 11x B. 6x + 5 C. 30 + 6x D. 6x + 30

  16. Select all expressions equivalent to 2x + 5x + 4. A. 7x + 4 B. (2 + 5)x + 4 C. 7x + 5 D. 11x

  17. Select all expressions equivalent to 4(x + 6). A. 10x B. 4x + 6 C. 4x + 24 D. 24 + 4x

  18. Let x = 3/2 and y = 2. Evaluate 5x + y². Part A: enter the value. Part B: show substitution and the order of operations.

  19. Select all expressions equal to 6³. A. 6 × 3 B. 6 × 6 × 6 C. 217 D. 216

  20. Evaluate 3² + 3(6 - 3). Part A: enter the value. Part B: a student begins by adding 3² and 3; explain why that changes the expression.

Answer key
  1. +28 m; 0 means at sea level

    Sea level is the reference. Below is negative and above is positive.

  2. 8

    -8 and 8 are each 8 units from 0 on opposite sides.

  3. -8 < 0 < 7; unit steps

    Negative -8 is left of or below 0; 7 is right of or above 0. Use equal intervals.

  4. -5 < 0 < 3; unit steps

    Negative -5 is left of or below 0; 3 is right of or above 0. Use equal intervals.

  5. 4 × 4 = 16

    The exponent counts 2 factors of 4; multiply all factors.

  6. 21

    Use parentheses and exponents first, then × and ÷ left to right, then + and -. Result: 21.

  7. 5

    Common factors: 1, 5; greatest is 5.

  8. 72

    The first positive multiple shared by 8 and 9 is 72.

  9. 12

    Common factors: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12; greatest is 12.

  10. 14(2 + 3)

    GCF = 14; 28 = 14 × 2 and 42 = 14 × 3.

  11. Outer quotient; dividend (5x + 7); divisor 2; inner sum 5x + 7

    The grouped sum is one complete quantity being divided. Within it, the terms are 5x and 7.

  12. 4x + 3y - 2

    Marker cost is 4x; folder cost is 3y. Add these subtotals, then subtract the one fixed coupon of 2. An expression does not need an equals sign.

  13. 13

    Substitute: 4 × (1) + 3². The product is 4 and the square is 9. Add to get 13.

  14. A, D - 42 + 7x; 7x + 42

    Distribute 7 to both terms to get 7x + 42. Reversing the addends gives the same value. The other expressions fail, for example at x = 0.

  15. C, D - 30 + 6x; 6x + 30

    Distribute 6 to both terms to get 6x + 30. Reversing the addends gives the same value. The other expressions fail, for example at x = 0.

  16. A, B - 7x + 4; (2 + 5)x + 4

    Combine coefficients of like x terms: 2 + 5 = 7. The constant 4 stays separate.

  17. C, D - 4x + 24; 24 + 4x

    Distribute 4 to both terms to get 4x + 24. Reversing the addends gives the same value. The other expressions fail, for example at x = 0.

  18. 23/2

    Substitute: 5 × (3/2) + 2². The product is 15/2 and the square is 4. Add to get 23/2.

  19. B, D - 6 × 6 × 6; 216

    The exponent 3 means three factors of 6; their product is 216. It does not mean 6 times 3.

  20. 18

    Parentheses: 6 - 3 = 3. Exponent: 3² = 9. Multiply: 3 × 3 = 9. Add: 9 + 9 = 18. The product must be completed before the final sum.

Day 061 / Unit 5: Test Solutions by Substitution

6.EE.B.5

I can test a proposed solution by substitution.

enVision Topic 4: Represent and Solve Equations and Inequalities | Spectrum companion Chapter 5

Replace the variable with the proposed value, compute each side, and compare. A number is a solution only when it makes the original equation true.

Worked example

Does x = 4 solve 3x + 6 = 18? Show substitution.

3 × 4 + 6 = 18; compare with 18.

Yes; left side = 18

Worked example

Select all values of x from the list that make x + 6 > 8 true. Explain why each rejected value fails. A. 3 B. 1 C. 2 D. 4

Subtract 6 conceptually: x must exceed 2. Equality at 2 fails because the inequality is strict.

A, D - 3; 4

  1. Does x = 6 solve 4x + 5 = 25? Show substitution.

  2. Does x = 5 solve 3x + 2 = 17? Show substitution.

  3. Does x = 4 solve 3x + 6 = 15? Show substitution.

  4. Does x = 4 solve 8x + 5 = 37? Show substitution.

  5. Does x = 5 solve 8x + 2 = 34? Show substitution.

  6. Does x = 3 solve 5x + 6 = 21? Show substitution.

  7. Does x = 4 solve 5x + 5 = 20? Show substitution.

  8. Select all values of x from the list that make x + 3 > 5 true. Explain why each rejected value fails. A. 3 B. 4 C. 1 D. 2

  9. Select all values of x from the list that make x + 6 > 10 true. Explain why each rejected value fails. A. 4 B. 6 C. 3 D. 5

  10. Does x = 2 solve 7x + 7 = 21? Show substitution.

Answer key
  1. No; left side = 29

    4 × 6 + 5 = 29; compare with 25.

  2. Yes; left side = 17

    3 × 5 + 2 = 17; compare with 17.

  3. No; left side = 18

    3 × 4 + 6 = 18; compare with 15.

  4. Yes; left side = 37

    8 × 4 + 5 = 37; compare with 37.

  5. No; left side = 42

    8 × 5 + 2 = 42; compare with 34.

  6. Yes; left side = 21

    5 × 3 + 6 = 21; compare with 21.

  7. No; left side = 25

    5 × 4 + 5 = 25; compare with 20.

  8. A, B - 3; 4

    Subtract 3 conceptually: x must exceed 2. Equality at 2 fails because the inequality is strict.

  9. B, D - 6; 5

    Subtract 6 conceptually: x must exceed 4. Equality at 4 fails because the inequality is strict.

  10. Yes; left side = 21

    7 × 2 + 7 = 21; compare with 21.

Day 062 / Unit 5: Solve Addition Equations

6.EE.B.7

I can solve x + p = q and check.

enVision Topic 4: Represent and Solve Equations and Inequalities | Spectrum companion Chapter 5

Subtract p from both sides to preserve equality. Then substitute the answer into the original equation. The value of the left side must equal the right side.

Worked example

Subtract 6 on both sides. Then x = 6; substitution gives 12 on each side.

x = 6

Answer key

    Subtract 2 on both sides. Then x = 2; substitution gives 4 on each side.

    Subtract 5 on both sides. Then x = 4; substitution gives 9 on each side.

  3. x = 3

    Subtract 5 on both sides. Then x = 3; substitution gives 8 on each side.

    Subtract 8 on both sides. Then x = 3/2; substitution gives 19/2 on each side.

  5. x = 1

    Subtract 3 on both sides. Then x = 1; substitution gives 4 on each side.

  6. x = 5/2

    Subtract 8 on both sides. Then x = 5/2; substitution gives 21/2 on each side.

    Subtract 2 on both sides. Then x = 3/2; substitution gives 7/2 on each side.

Day 067 / Unit 5: Write One-Step Equations from Context

6.EE.B.6, 6.EE.B.7

I can model a situation with a one-step equation and solve it.

enVision Topic 4: Represent and Solve Equations and Inequalities | Spectrum companion Chapter 5

Define the unknown quantity. Translate the relationship into an equation before calculating. Use the inverse operation and check whether the answer makes sense in the situation.

Worked example

A collection grows by 6 cards to 42 cards. Let x be the starting count. Write and solve an equation.

Subtract 6 from both sides; 36 + 6 = 42.

x + 6 = 42; x = 36 cards

Worked example

5 identical ropes have total length 15/2 meters. Part A: write and solve an equation for one rope length x. Part B: show a substitution check.

Total = number of ropes × one length. Divide both sides by 5; 15/2 ÷ 5 = 3/2. Check 5 × 3/2 = 15/2.

5x = 15/2; x = 3/2 meters

  1. A collection grows by 7 cards to 35 cards. Let x be the starting count. Write and solve an equation.

  2. A collection grows by 2 cards to 6 cards. Let x be the starting count. Write and solve an equation.

  3. A collection grows by 7 cards to 49 cards. Let x be the starting count. Write and solve an equation.

  4. 7 identical lengths of rope total 14 m. Let x be the length of one piece. Write and solve an equation, then check.

  5. 2 identical lengths of rope total 16 m. Let x be the length of one piece. Write and solve an equation, then check.

  6. 5 identical lengths of rope total 35 m. Let x be the length of one piece. Write and solve an equation, then check.

  7. 7 identical lengths of rope total 28 m. Let x be the length of one piece. Write and solve an equation, then check.

  8. 2 identical ropes have total length 3 meters. Part A: write and solve an equation for one rope length x. Part B: show a substitution check.

  9. 3 identical ropes have total length 21/2 meters. Part A: write and solve an equation for one rope length x. Part B: show a substitution check.

  10. 6 identical lengths of rope total 18 m. Let x be the length of one piece. Write and solve an equation, then check.

Answer key
  1. x + 7 = 35; x = 28 cards

    Subtract 7 from both sides; 28 + 7 = 35.

  2. x + 2 = 6; x = 4 cards

    Subtract 2 from both sides; 4 + 2 = 6.

  3. x + 7 = 49; x = 42 cards

    Subtract 7 from both sides; 42 + 7 = 49.

  4. 7x = 14; x = 2 m

    Divide the total by 7: 14 ÷ 7 = 2.

  5. 2x = 16; x = 8 m

    Divide the total by 2: 16 ÷ 2 = 8.

  6. 5x = 35; x = 7 m

    Divide the total by 5: 35 ÷ 5 = 7.

  7. 7x = 28; x = 4 m

    Divide the total by 7: 28 ÷ 7 = 4.

  8. 2x = 3; x = 3/2 meters

    Total = number of ropes × one length. Divide both sides by 2; 3 ÷ 2 = 3/2. Check 2 × 3/2 = 3.

  9. 3x = 21/2; x = 7/2 meters

    Total = number of ropes × one length. Divide both sides by 3; 21/2 ÷ 3 = 7/2. Check 3 × 7/2 = 21/2.

  10. 6x = 18; x = 3 m

    Divide the total by 6: 18 ÷ 6 = 3.

Day 068 / Unit 5: Write and Graph Inequalities

6.EE.B.8

I can write and graph a constraint using an inequality.

enVision Topic 4: Represent and Solve Equations and Inequalities | Spectrum companion Chapter 5

Use > or < for a strict bound and ≥ or ≤ when the endpoint is included. Graph a strict bound with an open circle; use a closed circle for an included endpoint. Shade the direction of all solutions.

Worked example

Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is more than 48. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.

“more than” means >; exclude the endpoint.

x > 48; open circle at 48, shade right

Worked example

A club needs at least 35 members. Select all true statements for its membership count n. A. 35 is an allowed membership count. B. 34 is an allowed membership count. C. n > 35 D. n ≥ 35

At least includes the boundary, so n ≥ 35. On a real number line use a closed point and shade right; actual member counts are whole numbers.

A, D - 35 is an allowed membership count.; n ≥ 35

  1. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is at most 49. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.

  2. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is at least 42. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.

  3. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is at least 35. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.

  4. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is at most 16. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.

  5. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is more than 8. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.

  6. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is more than 25. Label the endpoint and shade the solution ray.

  7. Let x be any real number. Write and graph: x is at least 24. Give one included value and explain whether the endpoint is included.

  8. A club needs at least 10 members. Select all true statements for its membership count n. A. n > 10 B. 10 is an allowed membership count. C. 9 is an allowed membership count. D. n ≥ 10

  9. A club needs at least 8 members. Select all true statements for its membership count n. A. 7 is an allowed membership count. B. n ≥ 8 C. 8 is an allowed membership count. D. n > 8

  10. A museum group must have at least 25 participants. Let p be the participant count. Write an inequality and graph its real-number solution set. State which solutions make sense as participant counts.

Answer key
  1. x ≤ 49; closed circle at 49, shade left

    “at most” means ≤; include the endpoint.

  2. x ≥ 42; closed circle at 42, shade right

    “at least” means ≥; include the endpoint.

  3. x ≥ 35; closed circle at 35, shade right

    “at least” means ≥; include the endpoint.

  4. x ≤ 16; closed circle at 16, shade left

    “at most” means ≤; include the endpoint.

  5. x > 8; open circle at 8, shade right

    “more than” means >; exclude the endpoint.

  6. x > 25; open circle at 25, shade right

    “more than” means >; exclude the endpoint.

  7. x ≥ 24; closed circle at 24, shade right

    “at least” means ≥; include the endpoint. Example solution: 25.

  8. B, D - 10 is an allowed membership count.; n ≥ 10

    At least includes the boundary, so n ≥ 10. On a real number line use a closed point and shade right; actual member counts are whole numbers.

  9. B, C - n ≥ 8; 8 is an allowed membership count.

    At least includes the boundary, so n ≥ 8. On a real number line use a closed point and shade right; actual member counts are whole numbers.

  10. p ≥ 25; closed circle at 25, shade right; participant counts are whole numbers 25, 26, …

    “at least” means ≥. The real-number ray has infinitely many solutions, but participant counts must be whole numbers.

Day 069 / Unit 5: Test Inequality Solutions

6.EE.B.5, 6.EE.B.8

I can test values and explain the many solutions of an inequality.

enVision Topic 4: Represent and Solve Equations and Inequalities | Spectrum companion Chapter 5

Substitute each value and decide whether the comparison is true. A continuous number-line ray represents infinitely many real-number solutions. A real situation may restrict useful solutions to whole numbers.

Worked example

Which values in {5, 6, 7} satisfy x ≤ 6? Test each by substitution.

Values below 6 work; the endpoint works too.

5, 6

Worked example

A club needs at least 18 members. Select all true statements for its membership count n. A. n ≥ 18 B. n > 18 C. 17 is an allowed membership count. D. 18 is an allowed membership count.

At least includes the boundary, so n ≥ 18. On a real number line use a closed point and shade right; actual member counts are whole numbers.

  3. Which values in {14, 15, 16} satisfy x < 15? Test each by substitution.

  4. Which values in {10, 11, 12} satisfy x ≤ 11? Test each by substitution.

  5. Which values in {5, 6, 7} satisfy x < 6? Test each by substitution.

  6. Which values in {8, 9, 10} satisfy x ≤ 9? Test each by substitution.

  7. Which values in {8, 9, 10} satisfy x < 9? Does the real-number inequality have only these solutions? Explain.

  8. A club needs at least 6 members. Select all true statements for its membership count n. A. n > 6 B. 5 is an allowed membership count. C. n ≥ 6 D. 6 is an allowed membership count.

  9. A club needs at least 10 members. Select all true statements for its membership count n. A. n > 10 B. 10 is an allowed membership count. C. 9 is an allowed membership count. D. n ≥ 10

  10. Which values in {10, 11, 12} satisfy x ≤ 11? Does the real-number inequality have only these solutions? Explain.

Answer key
  1. 6

    Values below 7 work; the endpoint does not work.

  2. 11, 12

    Values below 12 work; the endpoint works too.

  3. 14

    Values below 15 work; the endpoint does not work.

  4. 10, 11

    Values below 11 work; the endpoint works too.

  5. 5

    Values below 6 work; the endpoint does not work.

  6. 8, 9

    Values below 9 work; the endpoint works too.

  7. 8; no, infinitely many real values satisfy it

    Values below 9 work; the endpoint does not work.

  8. C, D - n ≥ 6; 6 is an allowed membership count.

    At least includes the boundary, so n ≥ 6. On a real number line use a closed point and shade right; actual member counts are whole numbers.

  9. B, D - 10 is an allowed membership count.; n ≥ 10

  10. 10, 11; no, infinitely many real values satisfy it

Day 072 / Unit 5: Identify Independent and Dependent Variables

6.EE.C.9

I can identify quantities that change together and write a rule.

enVision Topic 4: Represent and Solve Equations and Inequalities | Spectrum companion Chapter 5

The independent variable is the input you choose; the dependent variable changes with it. Define both and write the output in terms of the input. A table and graph must show the same rule and units.

Worked example

A shop charges $7 for each of n notebooks. Name the independent and dependent variables and write a cost rule.

The chosen notebook count is the input; multiply it by price and add any fixed fee to get the cost.

Independent n = notebooks; dependent C = cost; C = 7n

Worked example

A machine uses 4 liters per hour. Part A: complete the (hours, liters) table: (0, __), (1, __), (3, __). Part B: choose the independent variable and write an equation for L in terms of h. Plot your pairs.

Hours are the input. Multiply each hour value by 4 to obtain liters. Graph hours horizontally and liters vertically.

(0,0), (1,4), (3,12); independent h; L = 4h

  1. A shop charges $5 for each of n notebooks. Name the independent and dependent variables and write a cost rule.

  2. A shop charges $4 for each of n notebooks. Name the independent and dependent variables and write a cost rule.

  3. A shop charges $3 for each of n notebooks. Name the independent and dependent variables and write a cost rule.

  4. A shop charges $2 for each of n notebooks plus a single $3 delivery fee. Name the independent and dependent variables and write a cost rule.

  5. A shop charges $2 for each of n notebooks plus a single $4 delivery fee. Name the independent and dependent variables and write a cost rule.

  6. A shop charges $8 for each of n notebooks plus a single $2 delivery fee. Name the independent and dependent variables and write a cost rule.

  7. A shop charges $4 for each of n notebooks plus a single $3 delivery fee. Name the independent and dependent variables and write a cost rule. What does the cost at n = 0 mean in this model?

  8. A machine uses 6 liters per hour. Part A: complete the (hours, liters) table: (0, __), (1, __), (4, __). Part B: choose the independent variable and write an equation for L in terms of h. Plot your pairs.

  9. A machine uses 3 liters per hour. Part A: complete the (hours, liters) table: (0, __), (1, __), (4, __). Part B: choose the independent variable and write an equation for L in terms of h. Plot your pairs.

  10. A shop charges $5 for each of n notebooks plus a single $3 delivery fee. Name the independent and dependent variables and write a cost rule. What does the cost at n = 0 mean in this model?

Answer key

    The chosen notebook count is the input; multiply it by price and add any fixed fee to get the cost.

  2. Independent n = notebooks; dependent C = cost; C = 4n

    The chosen notebook count is the input; multiply it by price and add any fixed fee to get the cost.

    The chosen notebook count is the input; multiply it by price and add any fixed fee to get the cost.

  5. Independent n = notebooks; dependent C = cost; C = 2n + 4

    The chosen notebook count is the input; multiply it by price and add any fixed fee to get the cost.

  6. Independent n = notebooks; dependent C = cost; C = 8n + 2

    The chosen notebook count is the input; multiply it by price and add any fixed fee to get the cost.

  7. Independent n = notebooks; dependent C = cost; C = 4n + 3; C(0) = $3, the fee

    The chosen notebook count is the input; multiply it by price and add any fixed fee to get the cost.

  8. (0,0), (1,6), (4,24); independent h; L = 6h

    Hours are the input. Multiply each hour value by 6 to obtain liters. Graph hours horizontally and liters vertically.

  9. (0,0), (1,3), (4,12); independent h; L = 3h

  7. Independent n = notebooks; dependent C = cost; C = 8n + 3; C(0) = $3, the fee

    The chosen notebook count is the input; multiply it by price and add any fixed fee to get the cost.

  8. (0,0), (1,4), (3,12); independent h; L = 4h

    Hours are the input. Multiply each hour value by 4 to obtain liters. Graph hours horizontally and liters vertically.

  9. (0,0), (1,2), (5,10); independent h; L = 2h

    Hours are the input. Multiply each hour value by 2 to obtain liters. Graph hours horizontally and liters vertically.

  10. (0, 6); (1, 13); (2, 20); (3, 27); y increases by 7

    Substitute each input into y = 7x + 6; x is horizontal and y vertical.

Day 075 / Unit 5: Unit 5 Test

6.EE.A.1, 6.EE.A.2c, 6.NS.B.4a, 6.NS.B.4b, 6.EE.B.5, 6.EE.B.7, 6.EE.B.6, 6.EE.B.8, 6.EE.C.9

I can choose a model, solve accurately, explain my reasoning, and check the result.

enVision Topic 4: Represent and Solve Equations and Inequalities | Spectrum companion Chapter 5

  1. Write 3^2 as repeated multiplication and evaluate.

  2. Evaluate 8 + 5 × 2. Show the intermediate steps.

  3. Find the GCF of 10 and 15. Show factor pairs or prime factors.

  4. Find the LCM of 2 and 6 by listing multiples.

  5. Does x = 7 solve 3x + 7 = 25? Show substitution.

  6. Solve x + 3 = 5. Check in the original equation.

  7. Solve x - 7 = 5/2. Check in the original equation.

  8. Solve 7 × x = 21/2. Check in the original equation.

  9. Solve x + 4 = 15/2. Check in the original equation.

  10. Solve x ÷ 8 = 7/16. Check in the original equation.

  11. 7 identical lengths of rope total 28 m. Let x be the length of one piece. Write and solve an equation, then check.

  12. A club needs at least 12 members. Select all true statements for its membership count n. A. 11 is an allowed membership count. B. n > 12 C. 12 is an allowed membership count. D. n ≥ 12

  13. A club needs at least 15 members. Select all true statements for its membership count n. A. n > 15 B. 14 is an allowed membership count. C. n ≥ 15 D. 15 is an allowed membership count.

  14. A club needs at least 42 members. Select all true statements for its membership count n. A. n ≥ 42 B. 41 is an allowed membership count. C. 42 is an allowed membership count. D. n > 42

  15. A club needs at least 25 members. Select all true statements for its membership count n. A. n > 25 B. 24 is an allowed membership count. C. 25 is an allowed membership count. D. n ≥ 25

  16. A machine uses 4 liters per hour. Part A: complete the (hours, liters) table: (0, __), (1, __), (4, __). Part B: choose the independent variable and write an equation for L in terms of h. Plot your pairs.

  17. A machine uses 3 liters per hour. Part A: complete the (hours, liters) table: (0, __), (1, __), (6, __). Part B: choose the independent variable and write an equation for L in terms of h. Plot your pairs.

  18. A machine uses 6 liters per hour. Part A: complete the (hours, liters) table: (0, __), (1, __), (4, __). Part B: choose the independent variable and write an equation for L in terms of h. Plot your pairs.

  19. Select all values of x from the list that make x + 7 > 12 true. Explain why each rejected value fails. A. 5 B. 4 C. 6 D. 7

  20. Solve x + 5/2 = 7. Select every representation of the solution. A. 11/2 B. 18/4 C. 13/2 D. 9/2

Answer key
  1. 3 × 3 = 9

    The exponent counts 2 factors of 3; multiply all factors.

  2. 18

    Use parentheses and exponents first, then × and ÷ left to right, then + and -. Result: 18.

  3. 5

    Common factors: 1, 5; greatest is 5.

  4. 6

    The first positive multiple shared by 2 and 6 is 6.

  5. No; left side = 28

    3 × 7 + 7 = 28; compare with 25.

  6. x = 2

    Subtract 3 on both sides. Then x = 2; substitution gives 5 on each side.

  7. x = 19/2

    Add 7 on both sides. Then x = 19/2; substitution gives 5/2 on each side.

  8. x = 3/2

    Divide by 7 on both sides. Then x = 3/2; substitution gives 21/2 on each side.

  9. x = 7/2

    Subtract 4 on both sides. Then x = 7/2; substitution gives 15/2 on each side.

  10. x = 7/2

    Multiply by 8 on both sides. Then x = 7/2; substitution gives 7/16 on each side.

  11. 7x = 28; x = 4 m

    Divide the total by 7: 28 ÷ 7 = 4.

  12. C, D - 12 is an allowed membership count.; n ≥ 12

    At least includes the boundary, so n ≥ 12. On a real number line use a closed point and shade right; actual member counts are whole numbers.

  13. C, D - n ≥ 15; 15 is an allowed membership count.

    At least includes the boundary, so n ≥ 15. On a real number line use a closed point and shade right; actual member counts are whole numbers.

  14. A, C - n ≥ 42; 42 is an allowed membership count.

    At least includes the boundary, so n ≥ 42. On a real number line use a closed point and shade right; actual member counts are whole numbers.

  15. C, D - 25 is an allowed membership count.; n ≥ 25

    At least includes the boundary, so n ≥ 25. On a real number line use a closed point and shade right; actual member counts are whole numbers.

  16. (0,0), (1,4), (4,16); independent h; L = 4h

    Hours are the input. Multiply each hour value by 4 to obtain liters. Graph hours horizontally and liters vertically.

  17. (0,0), (1,3), (6,18); independent h; L = 3h

    Hours are the input. Multiply each hour value by 3 to obtain liters. Graph hours horizontally and liters vertically.

  18. (0,0), (1,6), (4,24); independent h; L = 6h

    Hours are the input. Multiply each hour value by 6 to obtain liters. Graph hours horizontally and liters vertically.

  19. C, D - 6; 7

    Subtract 7 conceptually: x must exceed 5. Equality at 5 fails because the inequality is strict.

  20. B, D - 18/4; 9/2

    Use the inverse operation on both sides. The solution is 9/2; the doubled numerator and denominator name the same value. Substitution verifies the solution.

Day 076 / Unit 6: Describe Ratios with Ordered Labels

6.RP.A.1

I can describe Ratios with Ordered Labels, and explain and check my reasoning.

enVision Topic 5: Understand and Use Ratios and Rates | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

A ratio compares two quantities multiplicatively. In a:b, a names the first quantity and b names the second. Divide both terms by a common factor to simplify, while keeping the labels and order. For a:b, the total is a+b equal parts. The first part is a/(a+b) of the total; a/b compares it to the second part. Write labels before choosing the denominator.

Worked example

A tray has 3 red tiles and 5 blue tiles. Write the red-to-blue ratio in simplest form.

Keep red first; divide 3:5 by 1.

3:5

Worked example

A bag contains 25 red tiles and 30 blue tiles. Select all true statements. A. The fraction of all tiles that are red is 5/11. B. The red-to-blue ratio is 5:11. C. The red-to-blue ratio is 5:6. D. There are 56 tiles in all.

Divide both color counts by 5. For the fraction of the whole, use total 55 as denominator; red-to-blue compares just the two colors.

A, C - The fraction of all tiles that are red is 5/11.; The red-to-blue ratio is 5:6.

  4. A collection has 25 green and 20 yellow counters. Write the fraction that is green.

  5. A tray has 25 red tiles and 25 blue tiles. Write the red-to-blue ratio in simplest form.

  6. A collection has 40 green and 20 yellow counters. Write the fraction that is green.

  7. A tray has 4 red tiles and 12 blue tiles. Write the red-to-blue ratio in simplest form and complete: there are ___ times as many red as blue.

  9. A bag contains 10 red tiles and 30 blue tiles. Select all true statements. A. The red-to-blue ratio is 2:8. B. The red-to-blue ratio is 2:6. C. The fraction of all tiles that are red is 1/4. D. There are 41 tiles in all.

  10. A collection has 18 green and 36 yellow counters. Write the fraction that is green and the green-to-yellow ratio in simplest form. Explain the different denominators.

Answer key
  1. 3:2

    Keep red first; divide 3:2 by 1.

  2. 2/5

    Total = 25; green fraction = 10/25 = 2/5.

  3. 5:4

    Keep red first; divide 5:4 by 1.

  4. 5/9

    Total = 45; green fraction = 25/45 = 5/9.

  5. 1:1

    Keep red first; divide 25:25 by 25.

  6. 2/3

    Total = 60; green fraction = 40/60 = 2/3.

  7. 1:3; 1/3 times as many

    Keep red first; divide 4:12 by 4.

  8. A, D - The red-to-blue ratio is 2:3.; The fraction of all tiles that are red is 2/5.

    Divide both color counts by 5. For the fraction of the whole, use total 25 as denominator; red-to-blue compares just the two colors.

  9. B, C - The red-to-blue ratio is 2:6.; The fraction of all tiles that are red is 1/4.

    Divide both color counts by 5. For the fraction of the whole, use total 40 as denominator; red-to-blue compares just the two colors.

  10. 1/3; 1/2 or 1:2; the whole includes both colors

    Total = 54; green fraction = 18/54 = 1/3.

Day 077 / Unit 6: Generate Equivalent Ratios

enVision Topic 5: Understand and Use Ratios and Rates | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

Worked example

12; 24

Equivalent ratios multiply both quantities by the same factor. A uses 2; B uses 3. C and D change only one scaled quantity.

Scale two ratios until one quantity matches, then compare the other quantity. The larger count alone is not enough: compare the amount per equal-sized batch or per one item.

Worked example

Mix B

Day 079 / Unit 6: Represent Ratios with Tape Diagrams

6.RP.A.3a

I can use equal-size tape sections to solve a ratio problem.

enVision Topic 5: Understand and Use Ratios and Rates | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

For a ratio a:b, draw a sections for one quantity and b sections for the other. A total covers a+b sections. Divide the total by a+b to find one section, then multiply by each part count.

Worked example

The ratio of oak trees to pine trees is 6:5. There are 66 trees altogether. Draw a tape diagram and find each count.

11 equal sections total; each section is 66 ÷ 11 = 6.

36 oaks and 30 pines

Worked example

The ratio of paper flags to fabric flags is 3:2. There are 20 flags. Part A: draw a tape diagram and enter each count. Part B: a student says there are 13 paper flags. Use your model to explain the error.

There are 5 equal sections. Each is 20 ÷ 5 = 4. Multiply the section counts by 4.

12 paper; 8 fabric; the claim is 1 paper flag too many.

  8. The ratio of paper flags to fabric flags is 4:4. There are 16 flags. Part A: draw a tape diagram and enter each count. Part B: a student says there are 9 paper flags. Use your model to explain the error.

  9. The ratio of paper flags to fabric flags is 7:5. There are 36 flags. Part A: draw a tape diagram and enter each count. Part B: a student says there are 22 paper flags. Use your model to explain the error.

  10. The ratio of oak trees to pine trees is 5:3. There are 32 trees altogether. Draw a tape diagram and find each count and the difference between the counts.

Answer key
  1. 4 oaks and 12 pines

    8 equal sections total; each section is 16 ÷ 8 = 2.

  2. 36 oaks and 18 pines

    9 equal sections total; each section is 54 ÷ 9 = 6.

  3. 10 oaks and 25 pines

    7 equal sections total; each section is 35 ÷ 7 = 5.

  4. 16 oaks and 12 pines

    14 equal sections total; each section is 28 ÷ 14 = 2.

  5. 30 oaks and 18 pines

    8 equal sections total; each section is 48 ÷ 8 = 6.

  6. 10 oaks and 14 pines

    12 equal sections total; each section is 24 ÷ 12 = 2.

  7. 24 oaks and 28 pines; difference 4

    13 equal sections total; each section is 52 ÷ 13 = 4.

  8. 8 paper; 8 fabric; the claim is 1 paper flag too many.

    There are 8 equal sections. Each is 16 ÷ 8 = 2. Multiply the section counts by 2.

  9. 21 paper; 15 fabric; the claim is 1 paper flag too many.

    There are 12 equal sections. Each is 36 ÷ 12 = 3. Multiply the section counts by 3.

  10. 20 oaks and 12 pines; difference 8

    8 equal sections total; each section is 32 ÷ 8 = 4.

Day 081 / Unit 6: Use Double Number Lines

6.RP.A.3a

I can represent a ratio on a double number line.

enVision Topic 5: Understand and Use Ratios and Rates | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

Place zero on both parallel number lines. Align a known pair above and below one tick. Add equal jumps on each line so aligned values remain in the same ratio.

Worked example

Draw aligned number lines for 3 liters of paint covering 5 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 6 batches. How much area do 18 liters cover?

18 ÷ 3 = 6 batches; 6 × 5 = 30.

30 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (3,5), (18,30)

Worked example

Draw a double number line for 5 liters of paint covering 4 m². Part A: align 0, 1, 2, and 4 batches. Part B: enter the area covered by 20 liters and explain why the scales can be different.

Each aligned jump adds 5 liters and 4 m². Both quantities scale by 4; the number lines measure different units.

Aligned pairs (0,0), (5,4), (10,8), (20,16); 16 m²

  1. Draw aligned number lines for 3 liters of paint covering 7 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 5 batches. How much area do 15 liters cover?

  2. Draw aligned number lines for 8 liters of paint covering 2 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 3 batches. How much area do 24 liters cover?

  3. Draw aligned number lines for 2 liters of paint covering 7 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 6 batches. How much area do 12 liters cover?

  4. Draw aligned number lines for 4 liters of paint covering 3 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 5 batches. How much area do 20 liters cover?

  5. Draw aligned number lines for 6 liters of paint covering 5 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 5 batches. How much area do 30 liters cover?

  6. Draw aligned number lines for 4 liters of paint covering 3 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 2 batches. How much area do 8 liters cover?

  7. Draw aligned number lines for 6 liters of paint covering 2 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 4 batches. How much area do 24 liters cover?

  8. Draw a double number line for 6 liters of paint covering 4 m². Part A: align 0, 1, 2, and 7 batches. Part B: enter the area covered by 42 liters and explain why the scales can be different.

  9. Draw a double number line for 7 liters of paint covering 6 m². Part A: align 0, 1, 2, and 6 batches. Part B: enter the area covered by 42 liters and explain why the scales can be different.

  10. Draw aligned number lines for 6 liters of paint covering 7 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 4 batches. How much area do 24 liters cover?

Answer key
  1. 35 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (3,7), (15,35)

    15 ÷ 3 = 5 batches; 5 × 7 = 35.

  2. 6 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (8,2), (24,6)

    24 ÷ 8 = 3 batches; 3 × 2 = 6.

  3. 42 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (2,7), (12,42)

    12 ÷ 2 = 6 batches; 6 × 7 = 42.

  4. 15 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (4,3), (20,15)

    20 ÷ 4 = 5 batches; 5 × 3 = 15.

  5. 25 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (6,5), (30,25)

    30 ÷ 6 = 5 batches; 5 × 5 = 25.

  6. 6 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (4,3), (8,6)

    8 ÷ 4 = 2 batches; 2 × 3 = 6.

  7. 8 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (6,2), (24,8)

    24 ÷ 6 = 4 batches; 4 × 2 = 8.

  8. Aligned pairs (0,0), (6,4), (12,8), (42,28); 28 m²

    Each aligned jump adds 6 liters and 4 m². Both quantities scale by 7; the number lines measure different units.

  9. Aligned pairs (0,0), (7,6), (14,12), (42,36); 36 m²

    Each aligned jump adds 7 liters and 6 m². Both quantities scale by 6; the number lines measure different units.

  10. 28 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (6,7), (24,28)

    24 ÷ 6 = 4 batches; 4 × 7 = 28.

Put the first quantity on the horizontal axis and the second on the vertical axis. Build a table, label units, and plot ordered pairs. A point (x,y) describes both quantities together.

Day 083 / Unit 6: Understand Rates and Unit Rates

6.RP.A.2, 6.RP.A.3b

I can find a unit rate and explain its units.

enVision Topic 5: Understand and Use Ratios and Rates | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

A unit rate compares an amount with one unit of another quantity. Divide the first quantity by the second. Write the units, such as dollars per notebook, so you know what the quotient means.

Worked example

5 identical notebooks cost $10. Find the cost per notebook.

Unit rate = 10 ÷ 5 = 2 dollars per notebook.

$2 per notebook

Worked example

6 notebooks cost $42. Select all statements at the same price per notebook. A. 5 notebooks cost $36. B. 5 notebooks cost $35. C. Each notebook costs $252. D. Each notebook costs $7.

Cost per notebook = 42 ÷ 6 = 7. Multiply $7 by the chosen notebook count.

B, D - 5 notebooks cost $35.; Each notebook costs $7.

  1. 6 identical notebooks cost $18. Find the cost per notebook.

  2. 4 identical notebooks cost $12. Find the cost per notebook.

  3. 7 identical notebooks cost $42. Find the cost per notebook.

  4. 7 identical notebooks cost $21. Find the cost per notebook.

  5. 2 identical notebooks cost $12. Find the cost per notebook.

  6. 6 identical notebooks cost $42. Find the cost per notebook.

  7. 7 identical notebooks cost $14. Find the cost per notebook and the cost of 10 notebooks at this rate.

  8. 6 notebooks cost $12. Select all statements at the same price per notebook. A. Each notebook costs $2. B. 4 notebooks cost $8. C. 4 notebooks cost $9. D. Each notebook costs $72.

  9. 5 notebooks cost $10. Select all statements at the same price per notebook. A. 5 notebooks cost $10. B. Each notebook costs $50. C. Each notebook costs $2. D. 5 notebooks cost $11.

  10. 6 identical notebooks cost $15. Find the cost per notebook and the cost of 9 notebooks at this rate.

Answer key
  1. $3 per notebook

    Unit rate = 18 ÷ 6 = 3 dollars per notebook.

  2. $3 per notebook

    Unit rate = 12 ÷ 4 = 3 dollars per notebook.

  3. $6 per notebook

    Unit rate = 42 ÷ 7 = 6 dollars per notebook.

  4. $3 per notebook

    Unit rate = 21 ÷ 7 = 3 dollars per notebook.

  5. $6 per notebook

    Unit rate = 12 ÷ 2 = 6 dollars per notebook.

  6. $7 per notebook

    Unit rate = 42 ÷ 6 = 7 dollars per notebook.

  7. $2 per notebook; $20

    Unit rate = 14 ÷ 7 = 2 dollars per notebook.

  8. A, B - Each notebook costs $2.; 4 notebooks cost $8.

    Cost per notebook = 12 ÷ 6 = 2. Multiply $2 by the chosen notebook count.

  9. A, C - 5 notebooks cost $10.; Each notebook costs $2.

    Cost per notebook = 10 ÷ 5 = 2. Multiply $2 by the chosen notebook count.

  10. $2.5 per notebook; $22.5

    Unit rate = 15 ÷ 6 = 2.5 dollars per notebook.

Day 084 / Unit 6: Compare Unit Rates and Prices

6.RP.A.3b

I can compare unit prices to find the better buy.

enVision Topic 5: Understand and Use Ratios and Rates | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

Divide total cost by the number of items or units. Compare costs for exactly one of the same unit. A lower package price does not always give a lower unit price.

Worked example

Pack A has 2 markers for $4; Pack B has 4 markers for $10. Find each unit price and the better buy.

Divide each price by its marker count; compare 2 with 2.5.

A $2/marker; B $2.5/marker; A is cheaper

Worked example

Store A sells 4 notebooks for $20. Store B sells 5 notebooks for $30. Part A: enter the unit price at each store. Part B: choose the better buy and explain why package totals alone are insufficient.

Divide each cost by its own notebook count: 20/4 = 5; 30/5 = 6. Compare the same one-notebook unit.

A: $5 each; B: $6 each; A is the better buy.

  1. Pack A has 4 markers for $16; Pack B has 7 markers for $31.5. Find each unit price and the better buy.

  2. Pack A has 3 markers for $24; Pack B has 5 markers for $42.5. Find each unit price and the better buy.

  3. Pack A has 5 markers for $20; Pack B has 9 markers for $40.5. Find each unit price and the better buy.

  4. Pack A has 2 markers for $3; Pack B has 5 markers for $10. Find each unit price and the better buy.

  5. Pack A has 5 markers for $5; Pack B has 7 markers for $10.5. Find each unit price and the better buy.

  6. Pack A has 2 markers for $8; Pack B has 6 markers for $27. Find each unit price and the better buy.

  7. Pack A has 4 markers for $4; Pack B has 9 markers for $13.5. Find each unit price and the better buy. How much would 36 markers cost using each pack size?

  8. Store A sells 3 notebooks for $9. Store B sells 4 notebooks for $16. Part A: enter the unit price at each store. Part B: choose the better buy and explain why package totals alone are insufficient.

  9. Store A sells 2 notebooks for $4. Store B sells 3 notebooks for $9. Part A: enter the unit price at each store. Part B: choose the better buy and explain why package totals alone are insufficient.

  10. Pack A has 5 markers for $15; Pack B has 11 markers for $38.5. Find each unit price and the better buy. How much would 55 markers cost using each pack size?

Answer key
  1. A $4/marker; B $4.5/marker; A is cheaper

    Divide each price by its marker count; compare 4 with 4.5.

  2. A $8/marker; B $8.5/marker; A is cheaper

    Divide each price by its marker count; compare 8 with 8.5.

  3. A $4/marker; B $4.5/marker; A is cheaper

    Divide each price by its marker count; compare 4 with 4.5.

  4. A $1.5/marker; B $2/marker; A is cheaper

    Divide each price by its marker count; compare 1.5 with 2.

  5. A $1/marker; B $1.5/marker; A is cheaper

    Divide each price by its marker count; compare 1 with 1.5.

  6. A $4/marker; B $4.5/marker; A is cheaper

    Divide each price by its marker count; compare 4 with 4.5.

  7. A $1/marker; B $1.5/marker; A is cheaper; A $36, B $54

    Divide each price by its marker count; compare 1 with 1.5.

  8. A: $3 each; B: $4 each; A is the better buy.

    Divide each cost by its own notebook count: 9/3 = 3; 16/4 = 4. Compare the same one-notebook unit.

  9. A: $2 each; B: $3 each; A is the better buy.

    Divide each cost by its own notebook count: 4/2 = 2; 9/3 = 3. Compare the same one-notebook unit.

  10. A $3/marker; B $3.5/marker; A is cheaper; A $165, B $192.5

    Divide each price by its marker count; compare 3 with 3.5.

Day 085 / Unit 6: Compare Rate Models and Units

6.RP.A.2, 6.RP.A.3b

I can compare Rate Models and Units, and explain and check my reasoning.

enVision Topic 5: Understand and Use Ratios and Rates | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

A unit rate compares an amount with one unit of another quantity. Divide the first quantity by the second. Write the units, such as dollars per notebook, so you know what the quotient means. Divide total cost by the number of items or units. Compare costs for exactly one of the same unit. A lower package price does not always give a lower unit price. At a constant speed, distance = speed × time. Divide distance by time to find speed, or distance by speed to find time. Keep units consistent before calculating.

Worked example

7 identical notebooks cost $49. Find the cost per notebook.

Unit rate = 49 ÷ 7 = 7 dollars per notebook.

$7 per notebook

Worked example

A bicycle travels 140 km in 4 hours at constant speed. Select all true statements. A. In 1 hour it travels 36 km. B. The speed is 35 km per hour. C. The speed is 144 km per hour. D. In 3 hours it travels 105 km.

Divide distance by time: 140 ÷ 4 = 35 km/h. Multiply this unit rate by 3 for the later distance.

B, D - The speed is 35 km per hour.; In 3 hours it travels 105 km.

  1. 4 identical notebooks cost $16. Find the cost per notebook.

  2. Pack A has 3 markers for $18; Pack B has 6 markers for $39. Find each unit price and the better buy.

  4. 4 identical notebooks cost $28. Find the cost per notebook.

  5. Pack A has 4 markers for $16; Pack B has 9 markers for $40.5. Find each unit price and the better buy.

  7. 2 identical notebooks cost $6. Find the cost per notebook and the cost of 5 notebooks at this rate.

  8. Store A sells 6 notebooks for $12. Store B sells 7 notebooks for $21. Part A: enter the unit price at each store. Part B: choose the better buy and explain why package totals alone are insufficient.

  9. A bicycle travels 70 km in 2 hours at constant speed. Select all true statements. A. The speed is 35 km per hour. B. In 4 hours it travels 140 km. C. In 1 hour it travels 36 km. D. The speed is 72 km per hour.

  10. 4 identical notebooks cost $14. Find the cost per notebook and the cost of 7 notebooks at this rate.

Answer key
  1. $4 per notebook

    Unit rate = 16 ÷ 4 = 4 dollars per notebook.

  2. A $6/marker; B $6.5/marker; A is cheaper

    Divide each price by its marker count; compare 6 with 6.5.

  3. 40 km/h

    Speed = 80 ÷ 2 = 40; distance = speed × time.

  4. $7 per notebook

    Unit rate = 28 ÷ 4 = 7 dollars per notebook.

  5. A $4/marker; B $4.5/marker; A is cheaper

    Divide each price by its marker count; compare 4 with 4.5.

  6. 3 hours

    Time = distance ÷ speed = 30 ÷ 10 = 3.

  7. $3 per notebook; $15

    Unit rate = 6 ÷ 2 = 3 dollars per notebook.

  8. A: $2 each; B: $3 each; A is the better buy.

    Divide each cost by its own notebook count: 12/6 = 2; 21/7 = 3. Compare the same one-notebook unit.

  9. A, B - The speed is 35 km per hour.; In 4 hours it travels 140 km.

    Divide distance by time: 70 ÷ 2 = 35 km/h. Multiply this unit rate by 4 for the later distance.

  10. $3.5 per notebook; $24.5

    Unit rate = 14 ÷ 4 = 3.5 dollars per notebook.

Day 086 / Unit 6: Solve Constant-Speed Problems

6.RP.A.3b

I can solve constant-speed problems using a rate per hour.

enVision Topic 5: Understand and Use Ratios and Rates | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

At a constant speed, distance = speed × time. Divide distance by time to find speed, or distance by speed to find time. Keep units consistent before calculating.

Worked example

A bus travels at 40 km/h. How long does 280 km take?

Time = distance ÷ speed = 280 ÷ 40 = 7.

7 hours

Worked example

A bicycle travels 180 km in 6 hours at constant speed. Select all true statements. A. The speed is 186 km per hour. B. In 5 hours it travels 150 km. C. The speed is 30 km per hour. D. In 1 hour it travels 31 km.

Divide distance by time: 180 ÷ 6 = 30 km/h. Multiply this unit rate by 5 for the later distance.

B, C - In 5 hours it travels 150 km.; The speed is 30 km per hour.

  1. A cyclist travels 60 km in 6 hours at constant speed. Find the speed.

  2. A bus travels at 20 km/h. How long does 140 km take?

  3. A cyclist travels 210 km in 7 hours at constant speed. Find the speed.

  4. A bus travels at 10 km/h. How long does 50 km take?

  5. A cyclist travels 200 km in 5 hours at constant speed. Find the speed.

  6. A bus travels at 40 km/h. How long does 200 km take?

  7. A cyclist travels 45 km in 3/2 hours at constant speed. Find the speed and the distance in 5 hours.

  8. A bicycle travels 50 km in 2 hours at constant speed. Select all true statements. A. The speed is 52 km per hour. B. In 5 hours it travels 125 km. C. The speed is 25 km per hour. D. In 1 hour it travels 26 km.

  9. A bicycle travels 40 km in 4 hours at constant speed. Select all true statements. A. The speed is 44 km per hour. B. In 5 hours it travels 50 km. C. In 1 hour it travels 11 km. D. The speed is 10 km per hour.

  10. A bus travels at 25 km/h. How long does 25 km take?

Answer key
  1. 10 km/h

    Speed = 60 ÷ 6 = 10; distance = speed × time.

  2. 7 hours

    Time = distance ÷ speed = 140 ÷ 20 = 7.

  3. 30 km/h

    Speed = 210 ÷ 7 = 30; distance = speed × time.

  4. 5 hours

    Time = distance ÷ speed = 50 ÷ 10 = 5.

  5. 40 km/h

    Speed = 200 ÷ 5 = 40; distance = speed × time.

  6. 5 hours

    Time = distance ÷ speed = 200 ÷ 40 = 5.

  7. 30 km/h; 150 km

    Speed = 45 ÷ 3/2 = 30; distance = speed × time.

  8. B, C - In 5 hours it travels 125 km.; The speed is 25 km per hour.

    Divide distance by time: 50 ÷ 2 = 25 km/h. Multiply this unit rate by 5 for the later distance.

  9. B, D - In 5 hours it travels 50 km.; The speed is 10 km per hour.

    Divide distance by time: 40 ÷ 4 = 10 km/h. Multiply this unit rate by 5 for the later distance.

  10. 1 hours

    Time = distance ÷ speed = 25 ÷ 25 = 1.

Day 087 / Unit 6: Convert Customary Units with Ratios

6.RP.A.3d

I can convert measurements using equivalent ratios.

enVision Topic 5: Understand and Use Ratios and Rates | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

Write an equality such as 1 foot = 12 inches. Multiply to change larger units into smaller units; divide for the reverse. A conversion factor is a ratio equal to 1, so the amount stays the same.

Worked example

Convert 4 yards to feet. Show the conversion ratio.

4 × 3 = 12. Smaller units require more units for the same amount.

12 feet

Worked example

Use 1 feet = 12 inches. Select all statements equivalent to 9/2 feet. A. 54 inches B. 3/8 inches C. 55 inches D. 9/2 × 12 inches

There are 12 smaller units in each larger one. Multiply 9/2 by 12, giving 54 inches. The physical length does not change.

A, D - 54 inches; 9/2 × 12 inches

  1. Convert 6 yards to feet. Show the conversion ratio.

  2. Convert 6 feet to inches. Show the conversion ratio.

  3. Convert 5 yards to feet. Show the conversion ratio.

  4. Convert 84 inches to feet. Show the conversion ratio.

  5. Convert 3 feet to inches. Show the conversion ratio.

  6. Convert 36 inches to feet. Show the conversion ratio.

  7. Convert 3/2 feet to inches. Show the conversion ratio and explain why the numerical value grows.

  8. Use 1 feet = 12 inches. Select all statements equivalent to 7/2 feet. A. 42 inches B. 43 inches C. 7/2 × 12 inches D. 7/24 inches

  9. Use 1 feet = 12 inches. Select all statements equivalent to 13/2 feet. A. 78 inches B. 79 inches C. 13/2 × 12 inches D. 13/24 inches

  10. Convert 6 feet to yards. Show the conversion ratio.

Answer key
  1. 18 feet

    6 × 3 = 18. Smaller units require more units for the same amount.

  2. 72 inches

    6 × 12 = 72. Smaller units require more units for the same amount.

  3. 15 feet

    5 × 3 = 15. Smaller units require more units for the same amount.

  4. 7 feet

    84 ÷ 12 = 7; 1 feet = 12 inches.

  5. 36 inches

    3 × 12 = 36. Smaller units require more units for the same amount.

  6. 3 feet

    36 ÷ 12 = 3; 1 feet = 12 inches.

  7. 18 inches

    3/2 × 12 = 18. Smaller units require more units for the same amount.

  8. A, C - 42 inches; 7/2 × 12 inches

    There are 12 smaller units in each larger one. Multiply 7/2 by 12, giving 42 inches. The physical length does not change.

  9. A, C - 78 inches; 13/2 × 12 inches

    There are 12 smaller units in each larger one. Multiply 13/2 by 12, giving 78 inches. The physical length does not change.

  10. 2 yards

    6 ÷ 3 = 2; 1 yards = 3 feet.

Day 089 / Unit 6: Apply Ratio and Rate Reasoning

6.RP.A.3a, 6.RP.A.3b, 6.RP.A.3d

I can apply Ratio and Rate Reasoning, and explain and check my reasoning.

enVision Topic 5: Understand and Use Ratios and Rates | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

Multiply both values in a ratio by the same scale factor. Organize pairs in a table and check that every row compares the same quantities in the same order. At a constant speed, distance = speed × time. Divide distance by time to find speed, or distance by speed to find time. Keep units consistent before calculating. Divide total cost by the number of items or units. Compare costs for exactly one of the same unit. A lower package price does not always give a lower unit price. Write an equality such as 1 foot = 12 inches. Multiply to change larger units into smaller units; divide for the reverse. A conversion factor is a ratio equal to 1, so the amount stays the same.

Worked example

4; 10

Worked example

Use 1 feet = 12 inches. Select all statements equivalent to 5/2 feet. A. 31 inches B. 5/24 inches C. 30 inches D. 5/2 × 12 inches

C, D - 30 inches; 5/2 × 12 inches

  1. A mix uses 6 cups of oats for 5 cups of seeds. Complete the table (oats, seeds): (6, 5), (12, ___), (18, ___).

  2. A bus travels at 25 km/h. How long does 175 km take?

  3. Pack A has 2 markers for $12; Pack B has 7 markers for $45.5. Find each unit price and the better buy.

  4. Convert 120 minutes to hours. Show the conversion ratio.

  5. A mix uses 7 cups of oats for 5 cups of seeds. Complete the table (oats, seeds): (7, 5), (14, ___), (21, ___).

  6. A bus travels at 40 km/h. How long does 160 km take?

  9. A recipe uses 7 cups oats for 4 cups seeds. Select all (oats, seeds) pairs that keep this ratio. A. (14, 9) B. (14, 8) C. (36, 20) D. (21, 12)

  10. A bus travels at 20 km/h. How long does 60 km take?

Answer key
  1. 10; 15

    Multiply both parts by 2 and by 3.

  2. 7 hours

    Time = distance ÷ speed = 175 ÷ 25 = 7.

  3. A $6/marker; B $6.5/marker; A is cheaper

    Divide each price by its marker count; compare 6 with 6.5.

    120 ÷ 60 = 2; 1 hours = 60 minutes.

  5. 10; 15

    Multiply both parts by 2 and by 3.

  6. 4 hours

    Time = distance ÷ speed = 160 ÷ 40 = 4.

  7. A $3.5/marker; B $4/marker; A is cheaper; A $49, B $56

    Divide each price by its marker count; compare 3.5 with 4.

  8. A, D - 54 inches; 9/2 × 12 inches

    There are 12 smaller units in each larger one. Multiply 9/2 by 12, giving 54 inches. The physical length does not change.

  9. B, D - (14, 8); (21, 12)

    Equivalent ratios multiply both quantities by the same factor. A uses 2; B uses 3. C and D change only one scaled quantity.

  10. 3 hours

    Time = distance ÷ speed = 60 ÷ 20 = 3.

Day 090 / Unit 6: Unit 6 Test

6.EE.B.5, 6.EE.B.7, 6.RP.A.1, 6.RP.A.3a, 6.RP.A.2, 6.RP.A.3b, 6.RP.A.3d
