  4. Solve 2 × x = 12. Check in the original equation.

  5. A tray has 7 red tiles and 2 blue tiles. Write the red-to-blue ratio in simplest form.

  6. A mix uses 6 cups of oats for 3 cups of seeds. Complete the table (oats, seeds): (6, 3), (12, ___), (36, ___).

  7. Mix A has 2 cups juice for 4 cups water. Mix B has 12 cups juice for 16 cups water. Which is stronger in juice? Show equivalent ratios.

  8. The ratio of oak trees to pine trees is 6:3. There are 45 trees altogether. Draw a tape diagram and find each count.

  9. A mix uses 7 cups of oats for 3 cups of seeds. Complete the table (oats, seeds): (7, 3), (14, ___), (14, ___).

  10. Draw aligned number lines for 3 liters of paint covering 6 square meters. Mark 0, one batch, and 5 batches. How much area do 15 liters cover?

  11. For every 6 tickets sold, a club earns $3. Make a table for 0, 1, 2, and 5 batches. Plot (tickets, dollars), label axes, and interpret (30, 15).

  12. 3 notebooks cost $18. Select all statements at the same price per notebook. A. 4 notebooks cost $24. B. 4 notebooks cost $25. C. Each notebook costs $54. D. Each notebook costs $6.

  13. Store A sells 5 notebooks for $20. Store B sells 6 notebooks for $30. Part A: enter the unit price at each store. Part B: choose the better buy and explain why package totals alone are insufficient.

  14. 3 notebooks cost $6. Select all statements at the same price per notebook. A. 2 notebooks cost $5. B. 2 notebooks cost $4. C. Each notebook costs $2. D. Each notebook costs $18.

  15. A bicycle travels 120 km in 4 hours at constant speed. Select all true statements. A. The speed is 124 km per hour. B. The speed is 30 km per hour. C. In 1 hour it travels 31 km. D. In 3 hours it travels 90 km.

  16. Use 1 feet = 12 inches. Select all statements equivalent to 5/2 feet. A. 31 inches B. 5/24 inches C. 30 inches D. 5/2 × 12 inches

  17. Use 1 meters = 100 centimeters. Select all statements equivalent to 5/2 meters. A. 1/40 centimeters B. 5/2 × 100 centimeters C. 250 centimeters D. 251 centimeters

  18. A bicycle travels 75 km in 3 hours at constant speed. Select all true statements. A. In 5 hours it travels 125 km. B. The speed is 25 km per hour. C. In 1 hour it travels 26 km. D. The speed is 78 km per hour.

  19. A bag contains 4 red tiles and 10 blue tiles. Select all true statements. A. There are 15 tiles in all. B. The fraction of all tiles that are red is 2/7. C. The red-to-blue ratio is 2:7. D. The red-to-blue ratio is 2:5.

  20. A recipe uses 5 cups oats for 2 cups seeds. Select all (oats, seeds) pairs that keep this ratio. A. (15, 6) B. (10, 5) C. (10, 4) D. (21, 8)

Answer key
  1. No; left side = 13

    2 × 3 + 7 = 13; compare with 11.

  2. x = 2

    Subtract 4 on both sides. Then x = 2; substitution gives 6 on each side.

  3. x = 21/2

    Add 8 on both sides. Then x = 21/2; substitution gives 5/2 on each side.

  4. x = 6

    Divide by 2 on both sides. Then x = 6; substitution gives 12 on each side.

  5. 7:2

    Keep red first; divide 7:2 by 1.

  6. 6; 18

    Multiply both parts by 2 and by 6.

  7. Mix B

    For 16 cups water, A uses 8 cups juice; B uses 12. B has more juice per equal water amount.

  8. 30 oaks and 15 pines

    9 equal sections total; each section is 45 ÷ 9 = 5.

  9. 6; 6

    Multiply both parts by 2 and by 2.

  10. 30 square meters; aligned pairs (0,0), (3,6), (15,30)

    15 ÷ 3 = 5 batches; 5 × 6 = 30.

  11. (0,0), (6,3), (12,6), (30,15); 30 tickets earns $15

    Scale both quantities by each batch count; plot horizontal tickets and vertical dollars.

  12. A, D - 4 notebooks cost $24.; Each notebook costs $6.

    Cost per notebook = 18 ÷ 3 = 6. Multiply $6 by the chosen notebook count.

  13. A: $4 each; B: $5 each; A is the better buy.

    Divide each cost by its own notebook count: 20/5 = 4; 30/6 = 5. Compare the same one-notebook unit.

  14. B, C - 2 notebooks cost $4.; Each notebook costs $2.

    Cost per notebook = 6 ÷ 3 = 2. Multiply $2 by the chosen notebook count.

  15. B, D - The speed is 30 km per hour.; In 3 hours it travels 90 km.

    Divide distance by time: 120 ÷ 4 = 30 km/h. Multiply this unit rate by 3 for the later distance.

  16. C, D - 30 inches; 5/2 × 12 inches

    There are 12 smaller units in each larger one. Multiply 5/2 by 12, giving 30 inches. The physical length does not change.

  17. B, C - 5/2 × 100 centimeters; 250 centimeters

    There are 100 smaller units in each larger one. Multiply 5/2 by 100, giving 250 centimeters. The physical length does not change.

  18. A, B - In 5 hours it travels 125 km.; The speed is 25 km per hour.

    Divide distance by time: 75 ÷ 3 = 25 km/h. Multiply this unit rate by 5 for the later distance.

  19. B, D - The fraction of all tiles that are red is 2/7.; The red-to-blue ratio is 2:5.

    Divide both color counts by 2. For the fraction of the whole, use total 14 as denominator; red-to-blue compares just the two colors.

  20. A, C - (15, 6); (10, 4)

    Equivalent ratios multiply both quantities by the same factor. A uses 2; B uses 3. C and D change only one scaled quantity.

Day 091 / Unit 7: Understand Percent as a Rate per 100

6.RP.A.3c

I can connect a percent with a fraction out of 100 and a decimal.

enVision Topic 6: Understand and Use Percent | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

Percent means per 100. Write p% as p/100, simplify the fraction, and divide by 100 to write a decimal. To turn a decimal into a percent, multiply by 100.

Worked example

Write 25% as a simplified fraction and decimal.

25% = 25/100 = 1/4; divide by 100 for 0.25.

1/4; 0.25

Worked example

Select all representations of 15%. A. 15/10 B. 0.15 C. 15 D. 15/100

Percent means per 100. Divide 15 by 100 to get 0.15; simplify 15/100 to 3/20.

B, D - 0.15; 15/100

  1. Write 10% as a simplified fraction and decimal.

  2. Write 20% as a simplified fraction and decimal.

  3. Write 50% as a simplified fraction and decimal.

  4. Write 40% as a simplified fraction and decimal.

  5. Write 30% as a simplified fraction and decimal.

  6. Write 15% as a simplified fraction and decimal.

  7. Write 75% as a simplified fraction and decimal.

  8. Select all representations of 75%. A. 75/100 B. 0.75 C. 75 D. 75/10

  9. Select all representations of 325%. A. 325/100 B. 325/10 C. 325 D. 3.25

  10. Write 60% as a simplified fraction and decimal.

Answer key
  1. 1/10; 0.1

    10% = 10/100 = 1/10; divide by 100 for 0.1.

  2. 1/5; 0.2

    20% = 20/100 = 1/5; divide by 100 for 0.2.

  3. 1/2; 0.5

    50% = 50/100 = 1/2; divide by 100 for 0.5.

  4. 2/5; 0.4

    40% = 40/100 = 2/5; divide by 100 for 0.4.

  5. 3/10; 0.3

    30% = 30/100 = 3/10; divide by 100 for 0.3.

  6. 3/20; 0.15

    15% = 15/100 = 3/20; divide by 100 for 0.15.

  7. 3/4; 0.75

    75% = 75/100 = 3/4; divide by 100 for 0.75.

  8. A, B - 75/100; 0.75

    Percent means per 100. Divide 75 by 100 to get 0.75; simplify 75/100 to 3/4.

  9. A, D - 325/100; 3.25

    Percent means per 100. Divide 325 by 100 to get 3.25; simplify 325/100 to 13/4.

  10. 3/5; 0.6

    60% = 60/100 = 3/5; divide by 100 for 0.6.

Day 093 / Unit 7: Represent Percents Above 100

6.RP.A.3c

I can connect a percent with a fraction out of 100 and a decimal.

enVision Topic 6: Understand and Use Percent | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

Percent means per 100. Write p% as p/100, simplify the fraction, and divide by 100 to write a decimal. To turn a decimal into a percent, multiply by 100.

Worked example

Write 170% as a simplified fraction and a decimal. Explain why its decimal is greater than 1.

170% = 170/100 = 17/10. Dividing by 100 gives 1.7, which exceeds 1 because 170 exceeds 100.

17/10; 1.7

Worked example

Select all representations of 375%. A. 375 B. 375/100 C. 375/10 D. 3.75

Percent means per 100. Divide 375 by 100 to get 3.75; simplify 375/100 to 15/4.

  1. Write 150% as a simplified fraction and a decimal. Explain why its decimal is greater than 1.

  2. Write 210% as a simplified fraction and a decimal. Explain why its decimal is greater than 1.

  4. Write 110% as a simplified fraction and a decimal. Explain why its decimal is greater than 1.

  5. Write 290% as a simplified fraction and a decimal. Explain why its decimal is greater than 1.

  6. Write 235% as a simplified fraction and a decimal. Explain why its decimal is greater than 1.

  7. Write 160% as a simplified fraction and a decimal. Explain why its decimal is greater than 1.

  8. Select all representations of 275%. A. 275 B. 275/100 C. 2.75 D. 275/10

  9. Select all representations of 325%. A. 325/100 B. 325/10 C. 325 D. 3.25

  10. Write 115% as a simplified fraction and a decimal. Explain why its decimal is greater than 1.

Answer key
  1. 3/2; 1.5

    150% = 150/100 = 3/2. Dividing by 100 gives 1.5, which exceeds 1 because 150 exceeds 100.

  2. 21/10; 2.1

    210% = 210/100 = 21/10. Dividing by 100 gives 2.1, which exceeds 1 because 210 exceeds 100.

  3. 31/20; 1.55

    155% = 155/100 = 31/20. Dividing by 100 gives 1.55, which exceeds 1 because 155 exceeds 100.

  4. 11/10; 1.1

    110% = 110/100 = 11/10. Dividing by 100 gives 1.1, which exceeds 1 because 110 exceeds 100.

  5. 29/10; 2.9

    290% = 290/100 = 29/10. Dividing by 100 gives 2.9, which exceeds 1 because 290 exceeds 100.

  6. 47/20; 2.35

    235% = 235/100 = 47/20. Dividing by 100 gives 2.35, which exceeds 1 because 235 exceeds 100.

  7. 8/5; 1.6

    160% = 160/100 = 8/5. Dividing by 100 gives 1.6, which exceeds 1 because 160 exceeds 100.

  8. B, C - 275/100; 2.75

    Percent means per 100. Divide 275 by 100 to get 2.75; simplify 275/100 to 11/4.

  9. A, D - 325/100; 3.25

    Percent means per 100. Divide 325 by 100 to get 3.25; simplify 325/100 to 13/4.

  10. 23/20; 1.15

    115% = 115/100 = 23/20. Dividing by 100 gives 1.15, which exceeds 1 because 115 exceeds 100.

Day 094 / Unit 7: Use Benchmark Percents

6.RP.A.3c

I can find the part when I know the percent and whole.

enVision Topic 6: Understand and Use Percent | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

Use part = (percent/100) × whole. You may also find 10%, 5%, or 1% and combine equal pieces. Label the part in the same units as the whole.

Worked example

Find 25% of 40.

25/100 × 40 = 10.

10

Worked example

A school club has 40 members; 15% take part in a project. Part A: enter the number of participants. Part B: write an equation and explain whether the answer is greater or less than the whole.

Rename 15% as 3/20. Multiply by 40 to obtain 6. Compare the rate with 1.

6 participants; n = (15/100) × 40; less than the whole because the percent is below 100

  1. Find 10% of 200.

  2. Find 50% of 180.

  3. Find 20% of 200.

  4. Find 125% of 100.

  5. Find 12% of 140.

  6. Find 5% of 220.

  7. Find 40% of 100. Then explain a mental-math or fraction strategy that checks your answer.

  8. A poster is enlarged to 125% of its original 100 cm width. Part A: enter the new width. Part B: explain why it exceeds the original.

  9. A school club has 80 members; 15% take part in a project. Part A: enter the number of participants. Part B: write an equation and explain whether the answer is greater or less than the whole.

  10. Find 125% of 100. Then explain a mental-math or fraction strategy that checks your answer.

Answer key
  1. 20

    10/100 × 200 = 20.

  2. 90

    50/100 × 180 = 90.

  3. 40

    20/100 × 200 = 40.

  4. 125

    125/100 × 100 = 125.

  5. 84/5

    12/100 × 140 = 84/5.

  6. 11

    5/100 × 220 = 11.

  7. 40

    40/100 × 100 = 40.

  8. 125 cm

    125/100 = 5/4 > 1. New width = 5/4 × 100 = 125 cm.

  9. 12 participants; n = (15/100) × 80; less than the whole because the percent is below 100

    Rename 15% as 3/20. Multiply by 80 to obtain 12. Compare the rate with 1.

  10. 125

    125/100 × 100 = 125.

Day 098 / Unit 7: Find the Whole from Part and Percent

6.RP.A.3c

I can find the whole when a part and percent are known.

enVision Topic 6: Understand and Use Percent | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

Write part = decimal rate × whole. Divide the part by the decimal rate to find the whole. Check by taking the stated percent of your answer.

Worked example

20% of a number is 16. Find the number and check.

Whole = 16 ÷ 1/5 = 80. Check 20% × 80 = 16.

80

Worked example

72 is 60% of an unknown quantity w. Select all equations that find w. A. (60/100)w = 72 B. w = 72 + 60 C. w = 72 ÷ (60/100) D. w = 72 × (60/100)

The part is percent rate times whole. Divide 72 by 3/5; w = 120. Check by multiplication.

A, C - (60/100)w = 72; w = 72 ÷ (60/100)

  1. 50% of a number is 70. Find the number and check.

  2. 10% of a number is 22. Find the number and check.

  3. 25% of a number is 40. Find the number and check.

  4. 25% of a number is 15. Find the number and check.

  5. 15% of a number is 27. Find the number and check.

  6. 75% of a number is 75. Find the number and check.

  7. 33 students are 15% of a school’s enrollment. Find the enrollment.

  8. 12 is 30% of an unknown quantity w. Select all equations that find w. A. (30/100)w = 12 B. w = 12 ÷ (30/100) C. w = 12 × (30/100) D. w = 12 + 30

  9. 60 is 60% of an unknown quantity w. Select all equations that find w. A. w = 60 ÷ (60/100) B. (60/100)w = 60 C. w = 60 × (60/100) D. w = 60 + 60

  10. 56 students are 40% of a school’s enrollment. Find the enrollment.

Answer key
  1. 140

    Whole = 70 ÷ 1/2 = 140. Check 50% × 140 = 70.

  2. 220

    Whole = 22 ÷ 1/10 = 220. Check 10% × 220 = 22.

  3. 160

    Whole = 40 ÷ 1/4 = 160. Check 25% × 160 = 40.

  4. 60

    Whole = 15 ÷ 1/4 = 60. Check 25% × 60 = 15.

  5. 180

    Whole = 27 ÷ 3/20 = 180. Check 15% × 180 = 27.

  6. 100

    Whole = 75 ÷ 3/4 = 100. Check 75% × 100 = 75.

  7. 220

    Whole = 33 ÷ 3/20 = 220. Check 15% × 220 = 33.

  8. A, B - (30/100)w = 12; w = 12 ÷ (30/100)

    The part is percent rate times whole. Divide 12 by 3/10; w = 40. Check by multiplication.

  9. A, B - w = 60 ÷ (60/100); (60/100)w = 60

    The part is percent rate times whole. Divide 60 by 3/5; w = 100. Check by multiplication.

  10. 140

    Whole = 56 ÷ 2/5 = 140. Check 40% × 140 = 56.

Day 099 / Unit 7: Check the Reference Whole

6.RP.A.3c

enVision Topic 6: Understand and Use Percent | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

  1. 20% of a number is 36. Find the number and check.

  6. 125% of a number is 200. Find the number and check.

  7. 48 students are 30% of a school’s enrollment. Find the enrollment.

  8. 24 is 60% of an unknown quantity w. Select all equations that find w. A. w = 24 × (60/100) B. w = 24 + 60 C. w = 24 ÷ (60/100) D. (60/100)w = 24

  9. 12 is 30% of an unknown quantity w. Select all equations that find w. A. (30/100)w = 12 B. w = 12 ÷ (30/100) C. w = 12 × (30/100) D. w = 12 + 30

  10. 80 students are 40% of a school’s enrollment. Find the enrollment.

Answer key
  1. 180

    Whole = 36 ÷ 1/5 = 180. Check 20% × 180 = 36.

  2. 60

    Whole = 30 ÷ 1/2 = 60. Check 50% × 60 = 30.

  3. 100

    Whole = 50 ÷ 1/2 = 100. Check 50% × 100 = 50.

  4. 240

    Whole = 144/5 ÷ 3/25 = 240. Check 12% × 240 = 144/5.

  5. 120

    Whole = 30 ÷ 1/4 = 120. Check 25% × 120 = 30.

  6. 160

    Whole = 200 ÷ 5/4 = 160. Check 125% × 160 = 200.

  7. 160

    Whole = 48 ÷ 3/10 = 160. Check 30% × 160 = 48.

  8. C, D - w = 24 ÷ (60/100); (60/100)w = 24

    The part is percent rate times whole. Divide 24 by 3/5; w = 40. Check by multiplication.

  9. A, B - (30/100)w = 12; w = 12 ÷ (30/100)

    The part is percent rate times whole. Divide 12 by 3/10; w = 40. Check by multiplication.

  10. 200

    Whole = 80 ÷ 2/5 = 200. Check 40% × 200 = 80.

Day 101 / Unit 7: Find the Unknown Percent

6.RP.A.3c

I can find the percent that one quantity is of another.

enVision Topic 6: Understand and Use Percent | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

Divide the part by the whole, then multiply by 100. Keep the same units in part and whole. The percent can be above 100 if the part is larger than the reference whole.

Worked example

70 is what percent of 140?

70 ÷ 140 × 100 = 50%; the reference whole is 140.

50%

Worked example

A quantity changes from a reference whole of 60 to a compared amount of 45. Part A: the compared amount is what percent of the reference whole? Part B: explain which number belongs in the denominator.

Percent = (45 ÷ 60) × 100 = 75%. The denominator is the reference whole, not automatically the larger number.

75%; denominator 60

  2. 12 is what percent of 120?

  3. 80 is what percent of 160?

  9. A quantity changes from a reference whole of 60 to a compared amount of 36. Part A: the compared amount is what percent of the reference whole? Part B: explain which number belongs in the denominator.

  10. 24 is what percent of 60? State which quantity is the reference whole.

Answer key
  1. 20%

    40 ÷ 200 × 100 = 20%; the reference whole is 200.

  2. 10%

    12 ÷ 120 × 100 = 10%; the reference whole is 120.

  3. 50%

    80 ÷ 160 × 100 = 50%; the reference whole is 160.

  4. 125%

    125 ÷ 100 × 100 = 125%; the reference whole is 100.

  5. 75%

    75 ÷ 100 × 100 = 75%; the reference whole is 100.

  6. 40%

    40 ÷ 100 × 100 = 40%; the reference whole is 100.

  7. 75%

    30 ÷ 40 × 100 = 75%; the reference whole is 40.

  8. 15%; denominator 100

    Percent = (15 ÷ 100) × 100 = 15%. The denominator is the reference whole, not automatically the larger number.

  9. 60%; denominator 60

    Percent = (36 ÷ 60) × 100 = 60%. The denominator is the reference whole, not automatically the larger number.

  10. 40%

    24 ÷ 60 × 100 = 40%; the reference whole is 60.

Day 105 / Unit 7: Unit 7 Test

6.RP.A.1, 6.RP.A.3a, 6.RP.A.3c

I can choose a model, solve accurately, explain my reasoning, and check the result.

enVision Topic 6: Understand and Use Percent | Spectrum companion Chapter 3

  1. A tray has 6 red tiles and 6 blue tiles. Write the red-to-blue ratio in simplest form.

  2. A mix uses 5 cups of oats for 6 cups of seeds. Complete the table (oats, seeds): (5, 6), (10, ___), (10, ___).

  3. Mix A has 5 cups juice for 6 cups water. Mix B has 14 cups juice for 12 cups water. Which is stronger in juice? Show equivalent ratios.

  4. The ratio of oak trees to pine trees is 2:6. There are 32 trees altogether. Draw a tape diagram and find each count.

  6. Write 10% as a simplified fraction and decimal.

  9. Write 5% as a simplified fraction and decimal.

  10. Find 15% of 160.

  13. 20 is 20% of an unknown quantity w. Select all equations that find w. A. w = 20 × (20/100) B. (20/100)w = 20 C. w = 20 + 20 D. w = 20 ÷ (20/100)

  14. 15 is 25% of an unknown quantity w. Select all equations that find w. A. w = 15 × (25/100) B. w = 15 + 25 C. w = 15 ÷ (25/100) D. (25/100)w = 15

  15. A quantity changes from a reference whole of 60 to a compared amount of 75. Part A: the compared amount is what percent of the reference whole? Part B: explain which number belongs in the denominator.

  18. 48 is 60% of an unknown quantity w. Select all equations that find w. A. (60/100)w = 48 B. w = 48 ÷ (60/100) C. w = 48 × (60/100) D. w = 48 + 60

  19. Select all representations of 250%. A. 250 B. 250/100 C. 250/10 D. 2.5

  20. Select all representations of 350%. A. 350/10 B. 350 C. 3.5 D. 350/100

Answer key
  1. 1:1

    Keep red first; divide 6:6 by 6.

  2. 12; 12

    Multiply both parts by 2 and by 2.

  3. Mix B

    For 12 cups water, A uses 10 cups juice; B uses 14. B has more juice per equal water amount.

  4. 8 oaks and 24 pines

    8 equal sections total; each section is 32 ÷ 8 = 4.

  5. 1/2; 0.5

    50% = 50/100 = 1/2; divide by 100 for 0.5.

  6. 1/10; 0.1

    10% = 10/100 = 1/10; divide by 100 for 0.1.

  7. 3; 3

    300% = 300/100 = 3. Dividing by 100 gives 3, which exceeds 1 because 300 exceeds 100.

  8. 40

    40/100 × 100 = 40.

  9. 1/20; 0.05

    5% = 5/100 = 1/20; divide by 100 for 0.05.

  10. 24

    15/100 × 160 = 24.

  11. 24

    40/100 × 60 = 24.

  12. C, D - (125/100)w = 100; w = 100 ÷ (125/100)

    The part is percent rate times whole. Divide 100 by 5/4; w = 80. Check by multiplication.

  13. B, D - (20/100)w = 20; w = 20 ÷ (20/100)

    The part is percent rate times whole. Divide 20 by 1/5; w = 100. Check by multiplication.

  14. C, D - w = 15 ÷ (25/100); (25/100)w = 15

    The part is percent rate times whole. Divide 15 by 1/4; w = 60. Check by multiplication.

  15. 125%; denominator 60

    Percent = (75 ÷ 60) × 100 = 125%. The denominator is the reference whole, not automatically the larger number.

  16. A, C - (15/100)w = 12; w = 12 ÷ (15/100)

    The part is percent rate times whole. Divide 12 by 3/20; w = 80. Check by multiplication.

  17. A, D - (40/100)w = 24; w = 24 ÷ (40/100)

    The part is percent rate times whole. Divide 24 by 2/5; w = 60. Check by multiplication.

  18. A, B - (60/100)w = 48; w = 48 ÷ (60/100)

    The part is percent rate times whole. Divide 48 by 3/5; w = 80. Check by multiplication.

  19. B, D - 250/100; 2.5

    Percent means per 100. Divide 250 by 100 to get 2.5; simplify 250/100 to 5/2.

  20. C, D - 3.5; 350/100

    Percent means per 100. Divide 350 by 100 to get 3.5; simplify 350/100 to 7/2.

Day 106 / Unit 8: Find Triangle Area

6.G.A.1

I can find the area of any triangle using a perpendicular height.

enVision Topic 7: Solve Area, Surface Area, and Volume Problems | Spectrum companion Chapter 6

Two matching triangles form a parallelogram, so A = (base × perpendicular height)/2. A slanted side is not necessarily the height. Label area in square units.

Worked example

Sketch a triangle with base 4 cm and perpendicular height 5 cm. Find its area.

A = bh/2 = 4 × 5 ÷ 2 = 10. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

10 cm²

Worked example

A triangle has base 14 cm and perpendicular height 4 cm. Select all expressions that give its area in cm². A. 7 × 4 B. (14 + 4) ÷ 2 C. 14 × 4 D. (14 × 4) ÷ 2

Triangle area is half the matching parallelogram: (14 × 4)/2 = 28 cm². Multiplying half the base by the height gives the same result.

A, D - 7 × 4; (14 × 4) ÷ 2

  1. Sketch a triangle with base 8 cm and perpendicular height 3 cm. Find its area.

  2. Sketch a triangle with base 16 cm and perpendicular height 5 cm. Find its area.

  3. Sketch a triangle with base 4 cm and perpendicular height 6 cm. Find its area.

  4. Sketch a triangle with base 14 cm and perpendicular height 5 cm. Find its area.

  5. Sketch a triangle with base 8 cm and perpendicular height 2 cm. Find its area.

  6. Sketch a triangle with base 12 cm and perpendicular height 5 cm. Find its area.

  7. Sketch a triangle with base 8 cm and perpendicular height 3/2 cm. Find its area. Explain why the slanted side is not needed.

  8. A triangle has base 8 cm and perpendicular height 6 cm. Select all expressions that give its area in cm². A. 8 × 6 B. 4 × 6 C. (8 × 6) ÷ 2 D. (8 + 6) ÷ 2

  9. A triangle has base 10 cm and perpendicular height 5 cm. Select all expressions that give its area in cm². A. (10 + 5) ÷ 2 B. 5 × 5 C. (10 × 5) ÷ 2 D. 10 × 5

  10. Sketch a triangle with base 10 cm and perpendicular height 7/2 cm. Find its area. Explain why the slanted side is not needed.

Answer key
  1. 12 cm²

    A = bh/2 = 8 × 3 ÷ 2 = 12. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

  2. 40 cm²

    A = bh/2 = 16 × 5 ÷ 2 = 40. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

  3. 12 cm²

    A = bh/2 = 4 × 6 ÷ 2 = 12. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

  4. 35 cm²

    A = bh/2 = 14 × 5 ÷ 2 = 35. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

  5. 8 cm²

    A = bh/2 = 8 × 2 ÷ 2 = 8. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

  6. 30 cm²

    A = bh/2 = 12 × 5 ÷ 2 = 30. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

  7. 6 cm²

    A = bh/2 = 8 × 3/2 ÷ 2 = 6. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

  8. B, C - 4 × 6; (8 × 6) ÷ 2

    Triangle area is half the matching parallelogram: (8 × 6)/2 = 24 cm². Multiplying half the base by the height gives the same result.

  9. B, C - 5 × 5; (10 × 5) ÷ 2

    Triangle area is half the matching parallelogram: (10 × 5)/2 = 25 cm². Multiplying half the base by the height gives the same result.

  10. 35/2 cm²

    A = bh/2 = 10 × 7/2 ÷ 2 = 35/2. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

Day 107 / Unit 8: Find Parallelogram and Trapezoid Area

6.G.A.1

I can find parallelogram and trapezoid areas by rearranging shapes.

enVision Topic 7: Solve Area, Surface Area, and Volume Problems | Spectrum companion Chapter 6

Cut and move a corner of a parallelogram to make a rectangle: A = bh. Two matching trapezoids form a parallelogram, giving A = (b1 + b2)h/2. Heights must be perpendicular.

Worked example

A parallelogram has base 2 m and perpendicular height 5 m. Sketch how it can be rearranged into a rectangle and find its area.

Cut and shift a corner; A = bh = 2 × 5 = 10.

10 m²

Worked example

A trapezoid has parallel bases 14 m and 22 m, and perpendicular height 3 m. Part A: enter its area. Part B: a student multiplies the longer base by the height; explain why that overcounts.

Use average base × height: (14 + 22)/2 × 3 = 54. A full rectangle at the longer base includes extra corner regions.

54 m²

  1. A parallelogram has base 8 m and perpendicular height 4 m. Sketch how it can be rearranged into a rectangle and find its area.

  2. A parallelogram has base 6 m and perpendicular height 4 m. Sketch how it can be rearranged into a rectangle and find its area.

  3. A parallelogram has base 8 m and perpendicular height 7 m. Sketch how it can be rearranged into a rectangle and find its area.

  4. A trapezoid has parallel bases 4 m and 14 m and perpendicular height 5 m. Sketch it and find its area.

  5. A trapezoid has parallel bases 8 m and 16 m and perpendicular height 7 m. Sketch it and find its area.

  6. A trapezoid has parallel bases 8 m and 12 m and perpendicular height 4 m. Sketch it and find its area.

  7. A trapezoid has parallel bases 6 m and 14 m and perpendicular height 5 m. Sketch it and find its area.

  8. A trapezoid has parallel bases 14 m and 22 m, and perpendicular height 4 m. Part A: enter its area. Part B: a student multiplies the longer base by the height; explain why that overcounts.

  9. A trapezoid has parallel bases 4 m and 8 m, and perpendicular height 2 m. Part A: enter its area. Part B: a student multiplies the longer base by the height; explain why that overcounts.

  10. A trapezoid has parallel bases 12 m and 24 m and perpendicular height 2 m. Sketch it and find its area.

Answer key
  1. 32 m²

    Cut and shift a corner; A = bh = 8 × 4 = 32.

  2. 24 m²

    Cut and shift a corner; A = bh = 6 × 4 = 24.

  3. 56 m²

    Cut and shift a corner; A = bh = 8 × 7 = 56.

  4. 45 m²

    A = (4 + 14) × 5 ÷ 2 = 45.

  5. 84 m²

    A = (8 + 16) × 7 ÷ 2 = 84.

  6. 40 m²

    A = (8 + 12) × 4 ÷ 2 = 40.

  7. 50 m²

    A = (6 + 14) × 5 ÷ 2 = 50.

  8. 72 m²

    Use average base × height: (14 + 22)/2 × 4 = 72. A full rectangle at the longer base includes extra corner regions.

  9. 12 m²

    Use average base × height: (4 + 8)/2 × 2 = 12. A full rectangle at the longer base includes extra corner regions.

  10. 36 m²

    A = (12 + 24) × 2 ÷ 2 = 36.

Day 108 / Unit 8: Decompose Composite Polygons

6.G.A.1

I can decompose a polygon into familiar shapes to find area.

enVision Topic 7: Solve Area, Surface Area, and Volume Problems | Spectrum companion Chapter 6

Sketch the figure and label every given length. Split the region into nonoverlapping rectangles and triangles, or subtract a cutout from a larger rectangle. Add only the areas that belong to the figure.

Worked example

An L-shaped garden is a 4 m by 4 m rectangle with an 2 m by 2 m corner rectangle removed. Sketch and find the garden area.

Outer area 16 minus cutout 4 = 12.

12 m²

Worked example

A 6 m by 10 m rectangle has a right-triangle corner removed with perpendicular legs 4 m and 6 m. Part A: enter the removed area. Part B: enter the remaining area and justify subtraction.

Day 109 / Unit 8: Use Coordinates to Find Polygon Lengths and Area

6.G.A.3, 6.G.A.1

I can draw polygons from coordinates and use side lengths to find area.

enVision Topic 7: Solve Area, Surface Area, and Volume Problems | Spectrum companion Chapter 6

Plot vertices in order and connect them, including the last to the first. Equal x-values make a vertical side; equal y-values make a horizontal side. Count or subtract coordinates to get the side lengths.

Worked example

Plot and connect (0,0), (13,0), (13,8), (0,8). Find the side lengths and area.

Horizontal length 13 - (0) = 13; vertical length 8 - (0) = 8; area = 13 × 8.

Sides 13 and 8; area 104 square units

Day 111 / Unit 8: Build and Interpret Nets

6.G.A.4

I can draw a net and account for every face of a solid.

enVision Topic 7: Solve Area, Surface Area, and Volume Problems | Spectrum companion Chapter 6

A net unfolds all faces into a plane without overlap. Rectangular prisms have three matching pairs of rectangles. Pyramids have a polygonal base and triangular sides. Add face areas, not edge lengths.

Worked example

Draw a net for a closed 7 × 6 × 2 cm rectangular prism. Label the six faces and calculate total area.

Add 2(42) + 2(14) + 2(12) = 136.

2 faces each of 7×6, 7×2, 6×2; 136 cm²

Worked example

A closed rectangular prism is 7 cm by 2 cm by 2 cm. Select all true descriptions of its net. A. Two faces are 7 by 2. B. Its net contains exactly four triangles. C. Two faces are 7 by 2 and two are 2 by 2. D. Adding just the three distinct face areas counts every face.

A closed rectangular prism has 6 rectangular faces in 3 matching pairs. Count both copies of each face.

A, C - Two faces are 7 by 2.; Two faces are 7 by 2 and two are 2 by 2.

  1. Draw a net for a closed 4 × 4 × 4 cm rectangular prism. Label the six faces and calculate total area.

  2. Draw a net for a closed 8 × 3 × 2 cm rectangular prism. Label the six faces and calculate total area.

  3. Draw a net for a closed 7 × 5 × 2 cm rectangular prism. Label the six faces and calculate total area.

  4. Draw a net for a closed 3 × 7 × 5 cm rectangular prism. Label the six faces and calculate total area.

  5. Draw a net for a closed 8 × 7 × 4 cm rectangular prism. Label the six faces and calculate total area.

  6. Draw a net for a closed 2 × 4 × 3 cm rectangular prism. Label the six faces and calculate total area.

  7. Draw a net for a triangular prism. Its two congruent right-triangle bases have legs 3 cm and 4 cm and hypotenuse 5 cm; prism length is 4 cm. Label all faces and find surface area.

  8. A closed rectangular prism is 6 cm by 4 cm by 4 cm. Select all true descriptions of its net. A. Two faces are 6 by 4. B. Its net contains exactly four triangles. C. Two faces are 6 by 4 and two are 4 by 4. D. Adding just the three distinct face areas counts every face.

  9. A closed rectangular prism is 6 cm by 5 cm by 3 cm. Select all true descriptions of its net. A. Its net contains exactly four triangles. B. Adding just the three distinct face areas counts every face. C. Two faces are 6 by 5. D. Two faces are 6 by 3 and two are 5 by 3.

  10. Draw a net for a triangular prism. Its two congruent right-triangle bases have legs 3 cm and 4 cm and hypotenuse 5 cm; prism length is 6 cm. Label all faces and find surface area.

Answer key
  1. 2 faces each of 4×4, 4×4, 4×4; 96 cm²

    Add 2(16) + 2(16) + 2(16) = 96.

  2. 2 faces each of 8×3, 8×2, 3×2; 92 cm²

    Add 2(24) + 2(16) + 2(6) = 92.

  3. 2 faces each of 7×5, 7×2, 5×2; 118 cm²

    Add 2(35) + 2(14) + 2(10) = 118.

  4. 2 faces each of 3×7, 3×5, 7×5; 142 cm²

    Add 2(21) + 2(15) + 2(35) = 142.

  5. 2 faces each of 8×7, 8×4, 7×4; 232 cm²

    Add 2(56) + 2(32) + 2(28) = 232.

  6. 2 faces each of 2×4, 2×3, 4×3; 52 cm²

    Add 2(8) + 2(6) + 2(12) = 52.

  7. 2 triangles (area 6 each), rectangles 3×4, 4×4, 5×4; SA 60 cm²

    Triangle pair: 12 cm²; rectangles: (3+4+5) × 4 = 48; total 60.

  8. A, C - Two faces are 6 by 4.; Two faces are 6 by 4 and two are 4 by 4.

    A closed rectangular prism has 6 rectangular faces in 3 matching pairs. Count both copies of each face.

  9. C, D - Two faces are 6 by 5.; Two faces are 6 by 3 and two are 5 by 3.

    A closed rectangular prism has 6 rectangular faces in 3 matching pairs. Count both copies of each face.

  10. 2 triangles (area 6 each), rectangles 3×6, 4×6, 5×6; SA 84 cm²

    Triangle pair: 12 cm²; rectangles: (3+4+5) × 6 = 72; total 84.

Day 112 / Unit 8: Find Prism Surface Area

6.G.A.4

I can use a rectangular-prism net to find surface area.

enVision Topic 7: Solve Area, Surface Area, and Volume Problems | Spectrum companion Chapter 6

A closed rectangular prism has two faces of area lw, two of lh, and two of wh. Add the six face areas: SA = 2lw + 2lh + 2wh. If a face is missing, do not include it.

Worked example

A 4 cm by 2 cm base and 4 cm height form a closed box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.

Closed surface area = 2(8+16+8) = 64.

64 cm²

Worked example

A closed box is 3 cm by 6 cm by 4 cm. Part A: enter its surface area. Part B: the 3-by-6 top is removed; enter the remaining surface area and explain.

Closed area = 2(18+12+24) = 108. Subtract exactly one top face, 18, for the open box.

Answer key

    Closed surface area = 2(36+12+12) = 120. Remove the top area 36: 84.

Day 113 / Unit 8: Find Pyramid Surface Area

6.G.A.4

I can find pyramid surface area from its triangular faces and base.

enVision Topic 7: Solve Area, Surface Area, and Volume Problems | Spectrum companion Chapter 6

For a square pyramid, draw one square and four triangles. Each triangle has area base edge × face height / 2. The face height is measured on the triangular face, not vertically inside the solid.

Worked example

A square pyramid has base edge 6 cm. Each triangular face has perpendicular face height 6 cm. Draw a net and calculate total surface area.

Base = 36; each triangle = 6 × 6 ÷ 2 = 18; add four triangles to get 108.

108 cm²

Worked example

A square pyramid net has base side 8 cm and four triangular faces, each with perpendicular face height 7 cm. Part A: enter one triangular face area. Part B: enter the entire surface area.

One face = (8 × 7)/2 = 28. Four faces total 112. Add square base area 64.

Face 28 cm²; total 176 cm²

  1. A square pyramid has base edge 12 cm. Each triangular face has perpendicular face height 8 cm. Draw a net and calculate total surface area.

  8. A square pyramid net has base side 12 cm and four triangular faces, each with perpendicular face height 8 cm. Part A: enter one triangular face area. Part B: enter the entire surface area.

  9. A square pyramid net has base side 12 cm and four triangular faces, each with perpendicular face height 10 cm. Part A: enter one triangular face area. Part B: enter the entire surface area.

  10. A square pyramid has base edge 12 cm. Each triangular face has perpendicular face height 11 cm. Draw a net and calculate total surface area. Explain why the vertical pyramid height is not needed.

Answer key
  1. 336 cm²

    Base = 144; each triangle = 12 × 8 ÷ 2 = 48; add four triangles to get 336.

  2. 340 cm²

    Base = 100; each triangle = 10 × 12 ÷ 2 = 60; add four triangles to get 340.

  3. 456 cm²

    Base = 144; each triangle = 12 × 13 ÷ 2 = 78; add four triangles to get 456.

  4. 64 cm²

    Base = 16; each triangle = 4 × 6 ÷ 2 = 12; add four triangles to get 64.

  5. 192 cm²

    Base = 64; each triangle = 8 × 8 ÷ 2 = 32; add four triangles to get 192.

  6. 176 cm²

    Base = 64; each triangle = 8 × 7 ÷ 2 = 28; add four triangles to get 176.

  7. 120 cm²

    Base = 36; each triangle = 6 × 7 ÷ 2 = 21; add four triangles to get 120.

  8. Face 48 cm²; total 336 cm²

    One face = (12 × 8)/2 = 48. Four faces total 192. Add square base area 144.

  9. Face 60 cm²; total 384 cm²

    One face = (12 × 10)/2 = 60. Four faces total 240. Add square base area 144.

  10. 408 cm²

    Base = 144; each triangle = 12 × 11 ÷ 2 = 66; add four triangles to get 408.

Day 114 / Unit 8: Find Volume with Fractional Edge Lengths

6.G.A.2

I can calculate prism volume with fractional edge lengths.

enVision Topic 7: Solve Area, Surface Area, and Volume Problems | Spectrum companion Chapter 6

A rectangular prism has volume V = lwh = Bh, where B is base area. Fractional edge lengths still multiply. Use cubic units; surface area and volume measure different attributes.

Worked example

A rectangular prism has edges 6, 1, and 2 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.

B = 6 × 1 = 6 cm²; V = B × 2 = 12 cm³.

12 cm³

Worked example

A prism measures 13/2 m by 3 m by 3/2 m. Part A: enter its volume. Part B: how many cubes with edge 1/2 m fill it? Explain why the cube count differs from the cubic-meter volume.

Multiply edges: 13/2 × 3 × 3/2 = 117/4. The edge counts are 13, 6, 3, giving 234 cubes. Each cube is 1/8 m³; 234/8 = 117/4.

117/4 m³; 234 cubes

  1. A rectangular prism has edges 2, 2, and 5 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.

  2. A rectangular prism has edges 4, 1, and 5 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.

  3. A rectangular prism has edges 2, 3, and 3 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.

  4. A rectangular prism has edges 2 1/2, 2 1/2, and 2 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.

  5. A rectangular prism has edges 1 1/2, 2 1/2, and 3 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.

  6. A rectangular prism has edges 2, 1 1/2, and 5 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.

  7. A rectangular prism has edges 3, 2 1/2, and 1 cm. Sketch it and find its volume. Show both lwh and Bh.

  8. A prism measures 7/2 m by 3/2 m by 5/2 m. Part A: enter its volume. Part B: how many cubes with edge 1/2 m fill it? Explain why the cube count differs from the cubic-meter volume.

  9. A prism measures 15/2 m by 3/2 m by 1 m. Part A: enter its volume. Part B: how many cubes with edge 1/2 m fill it? Explain why the cube count differs from the cubic-meter volume.

  10. A rectangular prism has edges 1, 2 1/2, and 3 cm. Sketch it and find its volume. Show both lwh and Bh.

Answer key
  1. 20 cm³

    B = 2 × 2 = 4 cm²; V = B × 5 = 20 cm³.

  2. 20 cm³

    B = 4 × 1 = 4 cm²; V = B × 5 = 20 cm³.

  3. 18 cm³

    B = 2 × 3 = 6 cm²; V = B × 3 = 18 cm³.

  4. 25/2 cm³

    B = 5/2 × 5/2 = 25/4 cm²; V = B × 2 = 25/2 cm³.

  5. 45/4 cm³

    B = 3/2 × 5/2 = 15/4 cm²; V = B × 3 = 45/4 cm³.

  6. 15 cm³

    B = 2 × 3/2 = 3 cm²; V = B × 5 = 15 cm³.

  7. 15/2 cm³

    B = 3 × 5/2 = 15/2 cm²; V = B × 1 = 15/2 cm³.

  8. 105/8 m³; 105 cubes

    Multiply edges: 7/2 × 3/2 × 5/2 = 105/8. The edge counts are 7, 3, 5, giving 105 cubes. Each cube is 1/8 m³; 105/8 = 105/8.

  9. 45/4 m³; 90 cubes

    Multiply edges: 15/2 × 3/2 × 1 = 45/4. The edge counts are 15, 3, 2, giving 90 cubes. Each cube is 1/8 m³; 90/8 = 45/4.

  10. 15/2 cm³

    B = 1 × 5/2 = 5/2 cm²; V = B × 3 = 15/2 cm³.

  8. 14 m³; 112 cubes

  9. 48 cm²; open box 44 cm²

    Closed area = 2(4+10+10) = 48. Subtract exactly one top face, 4, for the open box.

  10. 9/2 cm³

    B = 2 × 3/2 = 3 cm²; V = B × 3/2 = 9/2 cm³.

Day 116 / Unit 8: Connect Fractional Unit Cubes and Volume

I can connect fractional unit cubes with a prism volume formula.

enVision Topic 7: Solve Area, Surface Area, and Volume Problems | Spectrum companion Chapter 6

For cubes with edge 1/n, each cube has volume 1/n^3 cubic unit. Divide each prism edge by 1/n to count cubes along it. Multiply the counts, then multiply by the volume of one small cube.

Worked example

126 cubes; each 1/8 cubic unit; prism 63/4 cubic units

Day 117 / Unit 8: Use Base Area to Find Prism Volume

6.G.A.2

I can calculate prism volume with fractional edge lengths.

enVision Topic 7: Solve Area, Surface Area, and Volume Problems | Spectrum companion Chapter 6

A rectangular prism has volume V = lwh = Bh, where B is base area. Fractional edge lengths still multiply. Use cubic units; surface area and volume measure different attributes.

Worked example

A rectangular prism has edges 4, 2 1/2, and 4 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.

B = 4 × 5/2 = 10 cm²; V = B × 4 = 40 cm³.

40 cm³

Worked example

A prism measures 9/2 m by 3/2 m by 3/2 m. Part A: enter its volume. Part B: how many cubes with edge 1/2 m fill it? Explain why the cube count differs from the cubic-meter volume.

81/8 m³; 81 cubes

  5. A rectangular prism has edges 2 1/2, 2 1/2, and 6 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.

  9. A prism measures 7/2 m by 3/2 m by 1 m. Part A: enter its volume. Part B: how many cubes with edge 1/2 m fill it? Explain why the cube count differs from the cubic-meter volume.

Answer key
  1. 147/2 cm³

    B = 7 × 7/2 = 49/2 cm²; V = B × 3 = 147/2 cm³.

  2. 56 cm³

    B = 7 × 2 = 14 cm²; V = B × 4 = 56 cm³.

  3. 9 cm³

    B = 2 × 3/2 = 3 cm²; V = B × 3 = 9 cm³.

  4. 18 cm³

    B = 2 × 3/2 = 3 cm²; V = B × 6 = 18 cm³.

  5. 75/2 cm³

    B = 5/2 × 5/2 = 25/4 cm²; V = B × 6 = 75/2 cm³.

  6. 18 cm³

    B = 3 × 1 = 3 cm²; V = B × 6 = 18 cm³.

  7. 8 cm³

    B = 4 × 1 = 4 cm²; V = B × 2 = 8 cm³.

  8. 15/2 m³; 60 cubes

  9. 21/4 m³; 42 cubes

  10. 10 cm³

    B = 5/2 × 2 = 5 cm²; V = B × 2 = 10 cm³.

Day 118 / Unit 8: Distinguish Area, Surface Area, and Volume

6.G.A.1, 6.G.A.4, 6.G.A.2

I can distinguish Area, Surface Area, and Volume, and explain and check my reasoning.

enVision Topic 7: Solve Area, Surface Area, and Volume Problems | Spectrum companion Chapter 6

Two matching triangles form a parallelogram, so A = (base × perpendicular height)/2. A slanted side is not necessarily the height. Label area in square units. A closed rectangular prism has two faces of area lw, two of lh, and two of wh. Add the six face areas: SA = 2lw + 2lh + 2wh. If a face is missing, do not include it. A rectangular prism has volume V = lwh = Bh, where B is base area. Fractional edge lengths still multiply. Use cubic units; surface area and volume measure different attributes.

Worked example

Sketch a triangle with base 8 cm and perpendicular height 5 cm. Find its area.

A = bh/2 = 8 × 5 ÷ 2 = 20. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

20 cm²

Worked example

A prism measures 11/2 m by 5/2 m by 3/2 m. Part A: enter its volume. Part B: how many cubes with edge 1/2 m fill it? Explain why the cube count differs from the cubic-meter volume.

Multiply edges: 11/2 × 5/2 × 3/2 = 165/8. The edge counts are 11, 5, 3, giving 165 cubes. Each cube is 1/8 m³; 165/8 = 165/8.

165/8 m³; 165 cubes

  4. Sketch a triangle with base 6 cm and perpendicular height 7 cm. Find its area.

  5. A 6 cm by 2 cm base and 3 cm height form a closed box. Draw its net and find the material area. Ignore tabs and overlap.

  6. A rectangular prism has edges 2, 1, and 3 cm. Sketch it and find its volume.

  8. A closed box is 5 cm by 3 cm by 4 cm. Part A: enter its surface area. Part B: the 5-by-3 top is removed; enter the remaining surface area and explain.

  9. A prism measures 5/2 m by 2 m by 3/2 m. Part A: enter its volume. Part B: how many cubes with edge 1/2 m fill it? Explain why the cube count differs from the cubic-meter volume.

  10. Sketch a triangle with base 4 cm and perpendicular height 3 cm. Find its area. Explain why the slanted side is not needed.

Answer key
  1. 28 cm²

    A = bh/2 = 8 × 7 ÷ 2 = 28. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

  2. 144 cm²

    Closed surface area = 2(28+28+16) = 144.

  3. 72 cm³

    B = 6 × 3 = 18 cm²; V = B × 4 = 72 cm³.

  4. 21 cm²

    A = bh/2 = 6 × 7 ÷ 2 = 21. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

  5. 72 cm²

    Closed surface area = 2(12+18+6) = 72.

  6. 6 cm³

    B = 2 × 1 = 2 cm²; V = B × 3 = 6 cm³.

  7. 35/2 cm²

    A = bh/2 = 10 × 7/2 ÷ 2 = 35/2. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

  8. 94 cm²; open box 79 cm²

    Closed area = 2(15+20+12) = 94. Subtract exactly one top face, 15, for the open box.

  9. 15/2 m³; 60 cubes

    Multiply edges: 5/2 × 2 × 3/2 = 15/2. The edge counts are 5, 4, 3, giving 60 cubes. Each cube is 1/8 m³; 60/8 = 15/2.

  10. 6 cm²

    A = bh/2 = 4 × 3 ÷ 2 = 6. Two such triangles make a parallelogram.

Day 119 / Unit 8: Model a Geometry Design

6.G.A.3, 6.G.A.1, 6.G.A.4, 6.G.A.2

I can model a Geometry Design, and explain and check my reasoning.

enVision Topic 7: Solve Area, Surface Area, and Volume Problems | Spectrum companion Chapter 6

Plot vertices in order and connect them, including the last to the first. Equal x-values make a vertical side; equal y-values make a horizontal side. Count or subtract coordinates to get the side lengths. Sketch the figure and label every given length. Split the region into nonoverlapping rectangles and triangles, or subtract a cutout from a larger rectangle. Add only the areas that belong to the figure. A closed rectangular prism has two faces of area lw, two of lh, and two of wh. Add the six face areas: SA = 2lw + 2lh + 2wh. If a face is missing, do not include it. A rectangular prism has volume V = lwh = Bh, where B is base area. Fractional edge lengths still multiply. Use cubic units; surface area and volume measure different attributes.

Worked example

Plot and connect (0,0), (7,0), (7,8), (0,8). Find the side lengths and area.

Horizontal length 7 - (0) = 7; vertical length 8 - (0) = 8; area = 7 × 8.

Sides 7 and 8; area 56 square units

  9. 11 by 8 units; area 88 square units

  1. 1/4; 0.25

    25% = 25/100 = 1/4; divide by 100 for 0.25.

  2. 1/10; 0.1

    10% = 10/100 = 1/10; divide by 100 for 0.1.

  3. 29/10; 2.9

    290% = 290/100 = 29/10. Dividing by 100 gives 2.9, which exceeds 1 because 290 exceeds 100.

  4. 50

    50/100 × 100 = 50.

  18. Removed 15 m²; remaining 95 m²

    Outer area = 110. Triangle = 5 × 6/2 = 15. Subtract the removed region exactly once.

  19. A, C - 4 × 3; (8 × 3) ÷ 2

  20. 30 m²

Day 121 / Unit 9: Recognize Statistical Questions

6.SP.A.1

I can recognize a statistical question and the expected variability.

enVision Topic 8: Display, Describe, and Summarize Data | Spectrum companion Chapter 7

A statistical question anticipates different answers in a collection of data. Name the group, the measured attribute, and the expected variation. A question about one fixed fact is not statistical.

Worked example

Is “What are the wingspans, in centimeters, of the birds observed at a feeder?” statistical? Explain the group, attribute, units, and variation.

The question anticipates a collection of measurements. Each bird can have a different wingspan. Use the same unit for every observation.

Yes; observed birds; wingspan; centimeters; wingspans can vary.

Worked example

A class studies a collection of seedlings. Select all statistical questions, and identify the expected variability. A. How does height vary among the seedlings? B. What is 6 + 2? C. What is the height in centimeters of each of the seedlings? D. How many centimeters are in 6 meters?

Different seedlings can have different height. The questions anticipate variation across a collection. The conversion and arithmetic questions have fixed answers.

A, C - How does height vary among the seedlings?; What is the height in centimeters of each of the seedlings?

  1. Is “How many pages did each student read last night?” statistical? Explain.

  2. Is “How many centimeters are in one meter?” statistical? Explain.

  3. Is “What are the heights of the plants in our garden?” statistical? Explain.

  4. Identify the group, attribute, and units in: How many minutes do students in our class sleep each night?

  5. Rewrite “How long is my pencil?” as a statistical question.

  6. A student asks only one person’s age once. Explain why “How old is this person today?” is not statistical.

  7. Write a statistical question about travel to school. Name the group, attribute, units, and why answers may vary.

  8. A class studies a collection of students. Select all statistical questions, and identify the expected variability. A. How many centimeters are in 4 meters? B. What is the reading time in minutes of each of the students? C. What is 4 + 5? D. How does reading time vary among the students?

  9. A class studies a collection of backpacks. Select all statistical questions, and identify the expected variability. A. How does mass vary among the backpacks? B. How many centimeters are in 3 meters? C. What is the mass in kilograms of each of the backpacks? D. What is 3 + 4?

  10. Critique: “A question is statistical whenever it contains numbers.” Give a counterexample and a corrected rule.

Answer key
  1. Yes; pages read vary across students.

    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.

  2. No; one fixed conversion, 100 cm.

    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.

  3. Yes; plant heights vary.

    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.

  4. Group: class; attribute: sleep duration; units: minutes.

    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.

  5. Example: What are the lengths in centimeters of the pencils in our classroom?

    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.

  6. It seeks one fixed value, rather than anticipating variation in a data collection.

    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.

  7. Example: How many minutes do students in our class take to reach school? Group: class; travel time in minutes varies with routes and travel methods.

    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.

  8. B, D - What is the reading time in minutes of each of the students?; How does reading time vary among the students?

    Different students can have different reading time. The questions anticipate variation across a collection. The conversion and arithmetic questions have fixed answers.

  9. A, C - How does mass vary among the backpacks?; What is the mass in kilograms of each of the backpacks?

    Different backpacks can have different mass. The questions anticipate variation across a collection. The conversion and arithmetic questions have fixed answers.

  10. Counterexample: How many sides does a triangle have? It has a fixed answer. A statistical question anticipates variability in a collection of data.

    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.

Day 122 / Unit 9: Summarize Data with Mean and Median

6.SP.B.5a, 6.SP.B.5b, 6.SP.B.5c

I can summarize Data with Mean and Median, and explain and check my reasoning.

enVision Topic 8: Display, Describe, and Summarize Data | Spectrum companion Chapter 7

The mean is a fair share: add all values and divide by the number of observations. Report the count and measurement units. A large unusual value can pull the mean toward it. Order the data first. For an odd count, choose the middle value. For an even count, average the two middle values. The median often resists an unusually high or low value better than the mean.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 5, 6, 6, 7, 7, 8. Report n, units, and mean.

Sum = 39; mean = 39 ÷ 6 = 13/2.

n = 6; minutes; mean 13/2 min

Worked example

Six students recorded reading times (minutes): 4, 6, 6, 8, 8, 10. Select all true statements. A. The median is always the largest observation. B. The median is 7 minutes. C. The median is 8 minutes. D. The median is the average of the 3rd and 4th ordered values.

Six ordered observations have middle positions 3 and 4, so median = (6 + 8)/2 = 7.

B, D - The median is 7 minutes.; The median is the average of the 3rd and 4th ordered values.

  1. Reading times in minutes are 4, 5, 5, 6, 6, 7. Report n, units, and mean.

  2. Reading times in minutes are 4, 5, 5, 6, 6, 7. Find the median. Show the middle positions.

  3. Reading times in minutes are 3, 4, 4, 5, 5, 6. Report n, units, and mean.

  4. Reading times in minutes are 8, 12, 12, 16, 16, 20. Find the median. Show the middle positions.

  5. Reading times in minutes are 5, 8, 8, 11, 11, 14. Report n, units, and mean.

  6. Reading times in minutes are 3, 6, 6, 9, 9, 12. Find the median. Show the middle positions.

  7. Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 5, 14. Report n, units, and mean. If one more student reads 6 minutes, find the new mean.

  8. Six students recorded reading times (minutes): 3, 8, 8, 13, 13, 18. Select all true statements. A. The median is the average of the 3rd and 4th ordered values. B. The median is 23/2 minutes. C. The median is always the largest observation. D. The median is 21/2 minutes.

  9. Six students recorded reading times (minutes): 3, 9, 9, 15, 15, 21. Part A: enter the mean. Part B: a seventh student reads 27 minutes. Enter the new mean and explain the denominator.

  10. Reading times in minutes are 7, 8, 8, 9, 10, 19. Find the median and mean. Explain the difference in this set.

Answer key
  1. n = 6; minutes; mean 11/2 min

    Sum = 33; mean = 33 ÷ 6 = 11/2.

  2. Median 11/2 min

    6 ordered values: average positions 3 and 4: (5 + 6) ÷ 2 = 11/2.

  3. n = 6; minutes; mean 9/2 min

    Sum = 27; mean = 27 ÷ 6 = 9/2.

  4. Median 14 min

    6 ordered values: average positions 3 and 4: (12 + 16) ÷ 2 = 14.

  5. n = 6; minutes; mean 19/2 min

    Sum = 57; mean = 57 ÷ 6 = 19/2.

  6. Median 15/2 min

    6 ordered values: average positions 3 and 4: (6 + 9) ÷ 2 = 15/2.

  7. n = 6; minutes; mean 31/6 min; new mean 37/7 min

    Sum = 31; mean = 31 ÷ 6 = 31/6. New sum = 37; divide by 7.

  8. A, D - The median is the average of the 3rd and 4th ordered values.; The median is 21/2 minutes.

    Six ordered observations have middle positions 3 and 4, so median = (8 + 13)/2 = 21/2.

  9. 12 min; new mean 99/7 min

    Original sum 72 divided by 6 gives 12. Add 27, then divide the new sum by 7 because there are now 7 observations.

  10. Median 17/2 min; mean 61/6 min; the high value pulls the mean upward

    6 ordered values: average positions 3 and 4: (8 + 9) ÷ 2 = 17/2.

Day 127 / Unit 9: Find Quartiles and Interquartile Range

6.SP.A.3, 6.SP.B.5c

I can calculate quartiles and interquartile range.

enVision Topic 8: Display, Describe, and Summarize Data | Spectrum companion Chapter 7

Sort the data. Find the median, then the median of each half for Q1 and Q3. For an odd count, exclude the overall median from both halves in this course. IQR = Q3 - Q1; range = maximum - minimum.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 4, 5. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.

Lower half [2, 3, 3] has median 3; upper half [4, 4, 5] has median 4. Subtract Q1 from Q3.

Q1 3, Q3 4; IQR 1 min; range 3 min

Worked example

Six students recorded reading times (minutes): 5, 11, 11, 17, 17, 23. Part A: enter Q1 and Q3. Part B: enter IQR and explain which portion of the data it describes.

Lower-half median is 11; upper-half median is 17. IQR = 17 - 11 = 6, the span of the middle half of the ordered data.

Q1 = 11; Q3 = 17; IQR = 6 min

  1. Reading times in minutes are 8, 9, 9, 10, 10, 11. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.

  2. Reading times in minutes are 3, 4, 4, 5, 5, 6. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.

  3. Reading times in minutes are 6, 7, 7, 8, 8, 9. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.

  4. Reading times in minutes are 5, 11, 11, 17, 17, 23. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.

  5. Reading times in minutes are 2, 5, 5, 8, 8, 11. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.

  6. Reading times in minutes are 2, 7, 7, 12, 12, 17. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range.

  7. Reading times in minutes are 5, 6, 6, 7, 8, 17. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range. Explain what IQR describes.

  8. Six students recorded reading times (minutes): 7, 11, 11, 15, 15, 19. Part A: enter Q1 and Q3. Part B: enter IQR and explain which portion of the data it describes.

  9. Six students recorded reading times (minutes): 6, 11, 11, 16, 16, 21. Part A: enter Q1 and Q3. Part B: enter IQR and explain which portion of the data it describes.

  10. Reading times in minutes are 3, 4, 4, 5, 6, 15. Find Q1, Q3, IQR, and range. Explain what IQR describes.

Answer key
  1. Q1 9, Q3 10; IQR 1 min; range 3 min

    Lower half [8, 9, 9] has median 9; upper half [10, 10, 11] has median 10. Subtract Q1 from Q3.

  2. Q1 4, Q3 5; IQR 1 min; range 3 min

    Lower half [3, 4, 4] has median 4; upper half [5, 5, 6] has median 5. Subtract Q1 from Q3.

  3. Q1 7, Q3 8; IQR 1 min; range 3 min

    Lower half [6, 7, 7] has median 7; upper half [8, 8, 9] has median 8. Subtract Q1 from Q3.

  4. Q1 11, Q3 17; IQR 6 min; range 18 min

    Lower half [5, 11, 11] has median 11; upper half [17, 17, 23] has median 17. Subtract Q1 from Q3.

  5. Q1 5, Q3 8; IQR 3 min; range 9 min

    Lower half [2, 5, 5] has median 5; upper half [8, 8, 11] has median 8. Subtract Q1 from Q3.

  6. Q1 7, Q3 12; IQR 5 min; range 15 min

    Lower half [2, 7, 7] has median 7; upper half [12, 12, 17] has median 12. Subtract Q1 from Q3.

  7. Q1 6, Q3 8; IQR 2 min; range 12 min; IQR is the span of the middle half

    Lower half [5, 6, 6] has median 6; upper half [7, 8, 17] has median 8. Subtract Q1 from Q3.

  8. Q1 = 11; Q3 = 15; IQR = 4 min

    Lower-half median is 11; upper-half median is 15. IQR = 15 - 11 = 4, the span of the middle half of the ordered data.

  9. Q1 = 11; Q3 = 16; IQR = 5 min

    Lower-half median is 11; upper-half median is 16. IQR = 16 - 11 = 5, the span of the middle half of the ordered data.

  10. Q1 4, Q3 6; IQR 2 min; range 12 min; IQR is the span of the middle half

    Lower half [3, 4, 4] has median 4; upper half [5, 6, 15] has median 6. Subtract Q1 from Q3.

Day 128 / Unit 9: Find Mean Absolute Deviation

6.SP.B.5c

I can calculate and interpret mean absolute deviation.

enVision Topic 8: Display, Describe, and Summarize Data | Spectrum companion Chapter 7

Find the mean. Calculate each value’s distance from the mean, add those distances, then divide by the number of observations. MAD describes a typical distance from the mean in the original units.

Worked example

Reading times in minutes are 6, 7, 7, 8, 8, 9. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations.

Distances: 3/2, 1/2, 1/2, 1/2, 1/2, 3/2; sum 5; divide by 6 = 5/6.

Mean 15/2 min; MAD 5/6 min

Worked example

Six students recorded reading times (minutes): 4, 7, 7, 10, 10, 13. Part A: enter the mean. Part B: enter the MAD and interpret it in minutes.

Mean = 51/6 = 17/2. Absolute deviations: 9/2, 3/2, 3/2, 3/2, 3/2, 9/2. Their sum divided by 6 is 5/2. This is the average distance from the mean.

Mean 17/2 min; MAD 5/2 min

  1. Reading times in minutes are 5, 6, 6, 7, 7, 8. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations.

  2. Reading times in minutes are 7, 8, 8, 9, 9, 10. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations.

  3. Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 4, 5. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations.

  4. Reading times in minutes are 3, 6, 6, 9, 9, 12. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations.

  5. Reading times in minutes are 5, 9, 9, 13, 13, 17. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations.

  6. Reading times in minutes are 8, 14, 14, 20, 20, 26. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations.

  7. Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 5, 14. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations. Interpret MAD in a sentence.

  8. Six students recorded reading times (minutes): 2, 8, 8, 14, 14, 20. Part A: enter the mean. Part B: enter the MAD and interpret it in minutes.

  9. Six students recorded reading times (minutes): 4, 6, 6, 8, 8, 10. Part A: enter the mean. Part B: enter the MAD and interpret it in minutes.

  10. Reading times in minutes are 5, 6, 6, 7, 8, 17. Calculate the mean and MAD. Show all absolute deviations. Interpret MAD in a sentence.

Answer key
  1. Mean 13/2 min; MAD 5/6 min

    Distances: 3/2, 1/2, 1/2, 1/2, 1/2, 3/2; sum 5; divide by 6 = 5/6.

  2. Mean 17/2 min; MAD 5/6 min

    Distances: 3/2, 1/2, 1/2, 1/2, 1/2, 3/2; sum 5; divide by 6 = 5/6.

  3. Mean 7/2 min; MAD 5/6 min

    Distances: 3/2, 1/2, 1/2, 1/2, 1/2, 3/2; sum 5; divide by 6 = 5/6.

  4. Mean 15/2 min; MAD 5/2 min

    Distances: 9/2, 3/2, 3/2, 3/2, 3/2, 9/2; sum 15; divide by 6 = 5/2.

  5. Mean 11 min; MAD 10/3 min

    Distances: 6, 2, 2, 2, 2, 6; sum 20; divide by 6 = 10/3.

  6. Mean 17 min; MAD 5 min

    Distances: 9, 3, 3, 3, 3, 9; sum 30; divide by 6 = 5.

  7. Mean 31/6 min; MAD 53/18 min; a typical distance from the mean is 53/18 min

    Distances: 19/6, 13/6, 13/6, 7/6, 1/6, 53/6; sum 53/3; divide by 6 = 53/18.

  8. Mean 11 min; MAD 5 min

    Mean = 66/6 = 11. Absolute deviations: 9, 3, 3, 3, 3, 9. Their sum divided by 6 is 5. This is the average distance from the mean.

  9. Mean 7 min; MAD 5/3 min

    Mean = 42/6 = 7. Absolute deviations: 3, 1, 1, 1, 1, 3. Their sum divided by 6 is 5/3. This is the average distance from the mean.

  10. Mean 49/6 min; MAD 53/18 min; a typical distance from the mean is 53/18 min

    Distances: 19/6, 13/6, 13/6, 7/6, 1/6, 53/6; sum 53/3; divide by 6 = 53/18.

Day 129 / Unit 9: Choose Appropriate Statistical Measures

6.SP.B.5d

I can choose a measure of center and explain why.

enVision Topic 8: Display, Describe, and Summarize Data | Spectrum companion Chapter 7

Use the shape and context. Mean with mean absolute deviation can describe fairly symmetric data. Median with interquartile range often describes skewed data or data with an unusual value more fairly.

Worked example

Day 133 / Unit 9: Compare Data Distributions

6.SP.A.2, 6.SP.B.5d

I can compare centers and variability in two data sets.

enVision Topic 8: Display, Describe, and Summarize Data | Spectrum companion Chapter 7

Compute comparable measures for both groups. Compare typical values and consistency using the same units. State what the data support without claiming that every member of one group exceeds every member of the other.

Worked example

Reading times (min): Group A [6, 7, 7, 8, 8, 9], Group B [8, 9, 9, 10, 10, 11]. Compare median and IQR. Which group reads longer typically? Which is more consistent?

Adding 2 shifts all values and the median by 2, but does not change Q3 - Q1.

A median 15/2, B median 19/2 min; both IQR 1 min. B is typically 2 min higher; equal middle-half variability.

Worked example

Six students recorded reading times (minutes): 2, 4, 4, 6, 6, 8. A second group has every corresponding time 5 minutes larger. Part A: compare medians. Part B: compare IQRs and justify without listing all new data.

Adding 5 shifts every position and both quartiles by 5. The median increases by 5; Q3 - Q1 remains 2.

First median 5; second 10 min; both IQR 2 min.

Day 134 / Unit 9: Plan and Explain a Statistical Investigation

6.SP.A.1, 6.SP.B.4, 6.SP.B.5a, 6.SP.B.5b, 6.SP.B.5c, 6.SP.B.5d

I can plan and Explain a Statistical Investigation, and explain and check my reasoning.

enVision Topic 8: Display, Describe, and Summarize Data | Spectrum companion Chapter 7

A statistical question anticipates different answers in a collection of data. Name the group, the measured attribute, and the expected variation. A question about one fixed fact is not statistical. Draw an equally spaced number line and stack one dot per observation above its value. Repeated values stack vertically. Add an axis label with units and check that the dot count equals the data count. The mean is a fair share: add all values and divide by the number of observations. Report the count and measurement units. A large unusual value can pull the mean toward it. A useful summary names the group and attribute, units, observation count, center, variability, and shape. Explain why the chosen center and spread fit the data and mention any striking value.

Worked example

Is “What are the wingspans, in centimeters, of the birds observed at a feeder?” statistical? Explain the group, attribute, units, and variation.

The question anticipates a collection of measurements. Each bird can have a different wingspan. Use the same unit for every observation.

Yes; observed birds; wingspan; centimeters; wingspans can vary.

Worked example

Six students recorded reading times (minutes): 3, 6, 6, 9, 9, 12. Part A: describe center and spread using appropriate measures. Part B: describe shape, count, units, and any striking value; justify the chosen measures.

Order the observations. Middle positions give median 15/2 and half medians give IQR 3. Mean is 45/6; averaging distances gives MAD 5/2. Tie the chosen summary to the displayed shape and context.

6 observations; minutes; median 15/2, IQR 3; mean 15/2, MAD 5/2. Roughly balanced; mean/MAD or median/IQR with justification.

  1. Is “How many pages did each student read last night?” statistical? Explain.

  2. Reading times in minutes are 7, 8, 8, 9, 9, 10. Draw a labeled dot plot. Check the number of dots.

  3. Reading times in minutes are 6, 7, 7, 8, 8, 9. Report n, units, and mean.

  4. Reading times in minutes are 2, 7, 7, 12, 12, 17. Write a summary with count, attribute, units, center, variability, shape, and a justified choice of measures.

  5. Rewrite “How long is my pencil?” as a statistical question.

  6. Reading times in minutes are 3, 8, 8, 13, 13, 18. Draw a labeled dot plot. Check the number of dots.

  7. Reading times in minutes are 4, 5, 5, 6, 7, 16. Report n, units, and mean. If one more student reads 8 minutes, find the new mean.

  8. Six students recorded reading times (minutes): 2, 5, 5, 8, 8, 11. Part A: describe center and spread using appropriate measures. Part B: describe shape, count, units, and any striking value; justify the chosen measures.

  9. A class studies a collection of backpacks. Select all statistical questions, and identify the expected variability. A. How does mass vary among the backpacks? B. How many centimeters are in 5 meters? C. What is the mass in kilograms of each of the backpacks? D. What is 5 + 3?

  10. Reading times in minutes are 2, 3, 3, 4, 5, 14. Draw a labeled dot plot. Describe a cluster or gap and report the count.

Answer key
  1. Yes; pages read vary across students.

    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.

  2. 7: 1 dot; 8: 2 dots; 9: 2 dots; 10: 1 dot; n = 6

    Each observation contributes one dot; stack repeated values at their number-line position.

  3. n = 6; minutes; mean 15/2 min

    Sum = 45; mean = 45 ÷ 6 = 15/2.

  4. 6 observations of reading time in minutes. Mean 19/2 min, MAD 25/6 min; roughly symmetric, so mean/MAD are reasonable.

    Calculate the center and spread before interpreting them in context. Other accurate, justified summaries are acceptable.

  5. Example: What are the lengths in centimeters of the pencils in our classroom?

    Look for a collection of data and anticipated variation; identify the population and variable.

  6. 3: 1 dot; 8: 2 dots; 13: 2 dots; 18: 1 dot; n = 6

    Each observation contributes one dot; stack repeated values at their number-line position.

  7. n = 6; minutes; mean 43/6 min; new mean 51/7 min

    Sum = 43; mean = 43 ÷ 6 = 43/6. New sum = 51; divide by 7.

  8. 6 observations; minutes; median 13/2, IQR 3; mean 13/2, MAD 5/2. Roughly balanced; mean/MAD or median/IQR with justification.

    Order the observations. Middle positions give median 13/2 and half medians give IQR 3. Mean is 39/6; averaging distances gives MAD 5/2. Tie the chosen summary to the displayed shape and context.

  9. A, C - How does mass vary among the backpacks?; What is the mass in kilograms of each of the backpacks?

    Different backpacks can have different mass. The questions anticipate variation across a collection. The conversion and arithmetic questions have fixed answers.

  10. 2: 1 dot; 3: 2 dots; 4: 1 dot; 5: 1 dot; 14: 1 dot; n = 6; low cluster and high isolated value

    Each observation contributes one dot; stack repeated values at their number-line position.

Day 135 / Unit 9: Unit 9 Test

6.G.A.1, 6.G.A.3, 6.SP.A.1, 6.SP.B.5a, 6.SP.B.5b, 6.SP.B.5c, 6.SP.B.4, 6.SP.A.3, 6.SP.B.5d, 6.SP.A.2

  1. Sketch a triangle with base 8 cm and perpendicular height 4 cm. Find its area.
